

=== Introduction to the Essential Skills ===


--- Developing People, Leading Teams & Managing Processes ---

(gentle music)
-: Hi folks, my name is Larry Miller.
Welcome to my course on the New Manager,
the Essentials of Managing People, Teams, and Processes.
Congratulations, if you become a new manager.
Maybe I should say condolences,
because you know in your old world
before you were promoted to a manager,
you had a job and you knew what it was
and you knew how to get it done
and you were in control of it.
It was just about your behavior,
your motivation, your skills.
And now, you're a manager of others.
And now life isn't so simple, is it?
On the one hand, you've entered the world
where there's immense opportunity to move up the ladder
to greater compensation, greater responsibility,
greater success all around.
However, the other side of that,
the condolences side of that,
is that now you are dependent on others.
Your success is only the degree to which you can cause
other people to do their jobs well.
And that's hard.
It's not easy.
I've been managing for about 40 years,
managing a company and managing teams of people,
developing people.
And I know it's hard and you don't always succeed.
Sometimes you can't win,
but you can maximize the probability of your success
with every individual and every team
if you have the fundamental skills,
none of which are rocket science,
none of which are that hard.
But there are frames of mind,
there are ways of thinking about the job.
And there are very specific skills.
And, you know, skills are developed through action.
You know, if you're gonna learn to play the piano,
you can't just read a book and then say,
"Oh, I know how to play the piano."
No, you have to put your hands on the keys,
and you have to practice, practice, practice,
practice, practice, right?
Which is true if you're learning golf
or learning any sport, it's about behavior.
It's about you developing the automatic responses
that make you an effective manager.
So as we go through this course, I hope you have a coach.
Now, that coach may be the immediate manager above you,
or it may be a coach sort of on the side, if you will,
who isn't, you know, isn't the authority above you,
but as a helper, somebody who can give you feedback
and help you develop as a manager.
I believe in having a coach, so I hope you do have a coach.
But you and the coach are going to take action.
As we go through each section of the course,
I'm gonna prescribe, I'm gonna recommend,
I can't make you do it,
but I am gonna recommend some exercises,
some practice that will put the lessons to work.
And only by putting your hands on the actual work,
practicing, will you really become
good and effective as a manager.
And let's be honest, all of us who've been managing
for a long time know we don't have it all down.
I mean, it's a lifelong,
it's like becoming a good parent, right?
Or a good teacher or a good coach.
It's a lifelong experience.
There's always more you can learn.
So in this course, I'm trying to provide
the essentials of effective management,
but I expect that it's the beginning of a lifelong journey.
I hope it's the beginning of a lifelong journey for you.
Now, let me talk about the three major sections
of this course.
One is a focus on people and the skills of people,
motivating people, your communication skills.
There's a whole section we're gonna go through
on communication skills, listening skills,
giving and receiving feedback.
Very essential skills for a manager.
Learning to do that well.
And even the discipline issue, right?
The issue that none of us really wanna deal with.
But there are times that people don't perform,
even though we've given them feedback,
even though we've done a good job
of doing the things we should do to motivate them
and listen to them, communicate with them, et cetera.
Sometimes you have to engage in a process of discipline.
So, we'll go through all those things.
Each one of those is a subsection of the course.
Now, in the world that we live in today,
rarely do you just manage people one-on-one, individually.
And in fact, that's the hardest way to manage people.
Because if people are working together as a team,
they motivate each other and they teach each other,
they learn from each other,
they solve problems together as a team.
So I won't say it's just the easiest way to manage people,
but it is definitely the most effective way to
manage people in most organizations today
is to become an effective team leader.
So there's individual skill
that you're gonna help your individual employees develop,
individual motivation,
but now you're also gonna work
with a group of individuals as a team, I hope.
So we're gonna talk about team leadership.
There's a whole section on team leadership,
the stages of team development,
how to develop a team scorecard,
and, you know, if you look at any sport and you say,
"Well, what makes a team perform?"
They have a scorecard.
They know the score
and they're working towards the score, right?
And towards the championship,
towards some challenge, towards some goal.
Well, the same things are true
with working with a team at work.
You want them to have the challenge,
you want them to have the score,
you want them to have targets and objectives.
And we wanna talk about how you make decisions,
because there's individual decision making
and then there's team or group decision making.
And I want to help you to be effective in both.
And I want to help you figure out
when a decision should be an individual decision
and when should it be a team decision.
And then there is problem solving
and continuous improvement.
Every team, well, every individual really,
should be involved in lifelong learning for the individual
and continuous improvement,
continuously getting better as a team.
So I'm gonna give you a process of working with your team
to engage in continuous improvement
where they're always moving up a staircase,
improving data and improving their behavior,
improving their skills to achieve a challenge.
So we're gonna go through that whole process.
And I want you to think about process.
Because what do teams own?
What are they responsible for?
And what are you responsible for?
You're not just responsible for people,
you're responsible for a group of people getting work done.
And they get work done through a process.
In every process there's input and there's output.
You measure the effectiveness of the process
by the output of the process, right?
I mean, if you're a restaurant, for example,
there's input, incoming food stuff,
and incoming people who are gonna serve the food
and make the food and so forth.
Those are inputs into the process.
And then there's a process of cooking, right,
preparing the meal and serving the meal.
And the output is a customer who enjoys the meal
a good steak, (laughs)
a good, whatever the the dinner is right?
And a customer that's satisfied with that,
those are the outputs of that process.
So you as a manager are gonna be responsible
for managing a process.
And I'd like you just to think about something
Dr. Deming, the great guru of quality management,
said some years ago.
He said, "95% of the time
quality problems are in the process, not the person.
But 95% of the time we blame the person
and don't fix the process.
And then we can't figure out why the next person
isn't any good either," right?
Because they're working in a lousy process.
Well, you will succeed largely to the degree
that you are able to lead your team
in the improvement of processes.
So, you need to know what your processes,
you need to know who your team is, who's on your team,
what your team is responsible for,
and obviously you need to know who the people are, right?
So we're gonna go through all three of those things.
And there is leadership,
there's the development of people,
you lead teams, you develop people,
and you manage processes.
And we're gonna go through all three of those.
That's the challenge.
That's what the course is about.
Come on and join me, I think you'll enjoy it.
And I am quite confident you'll benefit from it
in improving your own performance as a manager.
(bright music)

--- What is Management, Leadership and Process? ---

(calm music)
-: Hi again, friends.
I'd like to define terms,
and particularly if you're a member of my circle
of influence here that a manager has, there are three ways
or three words that describe what a manager does
to influence people, process, and teams.
And those three words are leadership, management
and develop.
And I'd like to just explain them a little bit more
and talk about their relationship.
And if you look at my circles here, it's significant
and intentional that the circles overlap somewhat.
Because when you manage, develop, or lead a person,
you are also impacting the performance of the team.
When you develop a team and when you coach a team
and you motivate a team, that has influence,
of course, on the individual,
and both of those are what affect the process.
So there are, they are overlapping,
although we'll talk about them as we go through the course
somewhat separately, but there's always this overlap.
Lead, manage, and develop,
let's talk about those three terms.
Now, if you google the term management online
you'll find hundreds of definitions
if you keep looking, if you bother with that.
But let's just talk about the big picture
in terms of what that word connotes.
What do you hear when you hear the word manage?
And many people will hear the idea of controlling,
the idea of you deciding what other people should do
and making sure they do it, making sure they do it right.
Holding people accountable, you know,
that sort of idea of being in control.
That's not a great way to think about your job as a manager
because if you think about your job as manager
as simply telling and controlling
and deciding, you're gonna turn people off, frankly.
You're gonna demotivate people.
You're not gonna be effective as a team leader.
So you've got to do more than that.
And when we think about management,
let's just go down a list
of things that management does here,
and then let's talk about leadership
and developing people and teams.
One of your jobs is to utilize resources.
Now, those resources may be money, you may have a budget.
There's space in an office or a manufacturing plant
and they're people, they're the time of people.
Those are the critical resources that you have.
And your job is to use them
in the most productive possible way.
There's always this idea, which we'll talk about more later
of input and output, right?
Input of money, input of people, input of space,
and then you do something with it,
which is a process, right?
You utilize it and then there's an output.
The more output you can get for the less input
that's called efficiency or productivity, right?
So your job is to maximize the productivity
of your resources, of the time of your people,
the utilization of your space,
the utilization of your money, et cetera.
So that's one job of the manager.
People have energy.
Leadership creates human energy by inspiring people
and motivating and giving purpose.
The manager gives it direction
and says, "In order to achieve the purpose,
here's what we need to do.
We need to move in this direction."
Or by listening to people and engaging people,
involving people, defining processes well
and defining what we need to do well.
So you do assign tasks to people and you motivate people.
You do manage a process.
You know, you, I hope as a manager,
know what your process is, what's the input
the output points, and what that process is
that you're going to employ your team
to improve and to manage.
You engage people in continuous improvement.
We don't live in a static world.
Whatever we think the best way to do something is today,
and we may be right today, it'll be wrong tomorrow.
Why?
Because of new skills, new technology, new customer demands,
new changes in the external environment.
We'll talk more about that later.
But, the world's dynamic
and there's no business that escapes this.
I don't care whether you're, you know, a sales person
or whether you're in information technology,
developing software or you're in healthcare,
it doesn't matter.
We live in a very dynamic environment
and your job is to sense what's going on in the environment
and help people respond to that.
So there's a constant dynamic motion going on in the group
of people who you manage.
And your job is to sense that
and give it guidance and work with your team
and figuring out what the best way to adapt is.
And yes, there is an idea of control.
Processes can be in control or out of control.
But it's better to think about the process being in control
than the people being in control, right?
The people are the dynamic energy that you have to utilize.
If the more you try to control 'em or give a sense
of sort of authoritarian control, you drive out the energy.
So you don't want to do that.
What's the difference between management and leadership?
What does a leader do that a manager doesn't?
A manager is more tactical.
A manager is more into the detail
of specific tasks of schedules
and budgets and resources and you know, that sort of thing.
The leader says, "Why are we going where we're going?
Where are we going?
And why are we going there?
Why is it worthwhile to go there?"
A leader has a sense of purpose
and a sense of purpose that ennobles others.
Create a sense of purpose.
You do have a job, even though you're a manager,
you have a job of leadership too.
And that's it to inspire people with a worthwhile purpose
ask yourself the question, what do we as a company
what do we do as a unit?
Whatever our unit is, whatever we do,
what do we do that's value adding?
Why is it important? Who cares about it?
What's the real purpose of it?
You should think about that because you need
to communicate that to your team members, right?
And that way you create energy.
And if you're not able to talk about that,
if you're not aware of what our purpose is,
and why it's worthwhile, think about worthiness.
Why is what we do worthy?
What is the worthiness in what we do?
Because if you can't communicate that,
your people will lose energy.
The degree to which you can communicate that,
you create energy.
I hope that makes sense
and you can just sort of look at this list.
But this is, that's the very basic idea of what leaders do.
And I hope you will see your job as a leader.
You also have a development job
and that job is to develop the competencies of people.
I know you're not the training department, but you are too.
You know, yes, our parents, you know.
send their kids off to school.
But you know who really teaches them
more than the teachers in school?
It's mom and dad, right?
You know, the greatest influence in our life,
even though we may be very mature, is still our parents.
What we learn from mom and dad, that model.
And one of the jobs of leaders, by the way, versus managers
is to model the behavior that reflect our values.
So I hope you'll think about what are our values
and how do I model the behavior that reflect those values
that exhibit those values?
But development means training, it means coaching,
it means building skills.
We all need to, the reason you're taking this course
is to build your skills right?
Well, every employee
and every company needs to every day build their skills.
And your job as a manager is to help your subordinates,
to help your team members build skills
by giving them feedback, by helping them with training.
Maybe they're going through an online course,
and you can coach them in that online course.
You can go through it with them.
You can go, they can take this course
or they can take my team leadership course
or problem solving course.
And you can be their coach as they develop skills.
But they also develop skills as you engage them.
For example, we're gonna talk about problem solving.
As you engage them in problem solving,
they're gonna be learning to problem solve.
You know, if you brainstorm with them,
they're gonna learn how to brainstorm.
If you teach 'em about a Pareto diagram
and you do a Pareto analysis, they're gonna learn
about the 80/20 Rule, the Pareto analysis.
So we're constantly influencing our subordinates
by developing their competence.
And that's one of our central tasks.
What is a process?
We're gonna use that word a lot.
A process is a set of activities
that lead to a result, right?
And we're gonna map processes.
This is just a very sort of simple map,
but it says, you know, to make something, right?
Or to provide a service, to cook a meal.
You know, my restaurant example,
which is a nice simple one,
we can all think about, right?
There is input, there's output
and there's the process of how do you make stuff?
How do you cook a meal?
How do you, you know, how do you, what does a great chef do?
Well, there's steps in the process,
there's an order of things,
but they're individual tasks, right?
And the sum of those tasks is the process.
And there's a flow through that process.
And we are going to become experts in studying
and improving our process as we go through this course.
It's very central to being an effective manager today
no matter what field we're managing in
or what kind of an organization.
So I hope those four definitions are somewhat helpful
to you.
Let's come back and I wanna say a word
about culture and values.
Thanks.
(upbeat music)

=== Developing Employee Skills ===


--- The Heart of the Matter: Values and Culture ---

(light-hearted music)
-: Hi again, friends.
I wanna talk about values and vision,
and I know those are real soft sort of fuzzy words.
And I don't wanna sound like I'm preaching to you,
but I kind of wanna preach to you. (laughs)
I wanna try to convince you
to think about your values in particular.
Why? Because they affect everything.
They affect how people behave.
They affect people's motivation.
They affect whether people stay within boundaries
of acceptable behavior or go outside those boundaries.
It's all based on values.
It's all based on a sense of what's important.
And from the time we're children, you know,
we learn our values from our parents
but then from the school we go to and our friends.
And then when we go to work, you know,
the first job we have we have a sort of a sixth sense,
sort of radar, and we pick up what the values are
that are important.
What do people get rewarded for?
What pays off in this organization?
What's get punished in this organization?
And we're picking up values.
So it's important for you as a manager
to know what your values are as a manager,
and they should be aligned with the values of the company.
So you should know what your company's values are,
and if you don't, ask.
You know, ask your subordinate,
"What are the values that are important for me as a manager
to communicate to my team?"
'Cause you're gonna communicate values
to your team whether you intend to or not.
So, you know, back to my diagram here
which you'll be sick of.
At the heart of the matter, you know,
what really holds those three circles of people,
team, and process together is, what do we believe in?
You know, what are our values and where are we going?
Vision, where are we going?
Value is what's important, right?
So I'd like to talk about that just a little bit now.
Those things, you know, our vision, values,
and then the skills, competencies, motivation of people,
the effectiveness of teams,
and the effectiveness of our processes,
those sort of make up the organization culture.
And the organization culture is also affected
by the system structure, symbols in the organization.
In other words, system, who gets what information.
You know, that's a value statement, right?
Do we trust people with information?
Do we think they can make decisions based on information?
Do we give them the score?
Are we transparent in sharing what's really going on
with the company?
You know, those are systemic things,
the structure of the organization.
How many layers are there?
Are there 15 layers or three layers?
Boy, that really affects how people behave.
The more layers they are, decisions tend to get sucked up,
tends to disempower, you know, people at a lower level.
So generally, the fewer levels the better,
if people are trained to take responsibility at a low level,
which is one of the things this course is about.
And symbols, you know, why do we wear ties?
Who parks where, you know, who has offices
or doesn't have offices?
You know, those things,
all of which you may think are silly.
They're really not.
Because every culture,
if you just think about other cultures,
you know, native Americans or you know, Chinese culture
or Japanese culture, any other culture,
there are symbols, right?
Religious symbols, think about them.
Why do we have them?
Why do we care about them?
Why do we care about flags?
What's the flag about?
You know what the flag is about?
No matter what country you're from,
the flag is about the values of your country, right?
It's symbolic.
They're symbols.
They're not, you know, the cloth of a flag isn't a value
in and unto itself.
The real value comes from the symbolic nature of it, right?
And teams have symbols, right?
Well, why does every baseball team, you know,
if you're a fan of a baseball team, you have the cap
with the team's name or symbol, you know, on it.
And we like that. You know why?
'Cause it bonds people together.
Teams get bonded together if they have a common symbol.
So those things all affect the organization culture.
And there is a larger environment, right?
There's sort of concentric circles here.
There's you and your team there in the center,
and then there's the organization and it's culture.
But there's a larger environment.
There's a national culture, there are industry cultures.
I can tell you that as a consultant who's bounced around
from textile mills to steel mills to American Express
to other New York banks or big banks
to healthcare organizations, auto companies.
They all have different cultures based on the nature
of the business, based on the competitiveness
of the business.
So there is a larger culture of your industry
and of the country, the larger outer environment.
And those things are changing.
You know, those things are in motion.
And because those things are in motion,
they affect what goes on inside.
So if we go through an economic depression
or recession, you know, that affects us.
That affects our culture, that affects how we do things,
technological changes.
You know, I can tell you for sure,
one thing that you need to think about,
is every young person coming to work tomorrow
is internet savvy, has devices, right? (laughs)
You know, they have super iPhones.
They Google everything.
They have an expanded intelligence.
They have an expanded knowledge base.
Because it's not just what's in their head
it's what they can access and how fast they can access it.
And young people are very into that, right?
If you've taught in a classroom,
I've taught in college classrooms,
and it's sort of disturbing because, you know,
you're supposed to be the expert
and you're telling them what's what.
And they're sitting there with their laptop or their iPhone,
and they're Googling something
that you've just talked about.
And then they raise their hand and say, "Excuse me,
but you know, it's not exactly the date. It was this."
And you go, "Oh, great." (laughs)
You know, so everybody's connected to everybody else.
Everybody's connected to knowledge.
That's a technological reality that impacts us.
There are government changes, regulations,
particularly in healthcare and environmental things,
that impact our company.
So we as a manager need to be attuned to those things.
We need to recognize them
and be able to integrate them into our work,
understand how they're impacting them,
translate them for our team
so they know what to do about them.
Does that make sense?
I hope that makes sense just in thinking about,
you know, values.
And in the next lecture, let me come back
and very quickly talk about some values
that I think are really important
for almost any organization today.
Thanks.
(light-hearted music)

=== Motivating Your Team Members ===


--- Values Worth Considering ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let me just share with you a few values
that I think are important.
Now, you know, some values just should be obvious to you.
Absolute honesty and integrity.
Don't ever lie to your people.
Don't ever lie to your superior or parallel associates,
but you know that, don't you?
I don't need to tell you that, that's obvious.
But let me expand on a few values
that are maybe not so obvious.
You know,
there are two pillars of Toyota production system
or what we call "lean management".
One is respect for people.
The other is continuous improvement.
And they're values.
What does respect for people mean?
What does it really mean?
Let me give you an example.
I was involved at Honda America Manufacturing
in the early days when they came to the United States
and set up their plant in Marysville, Ohio
and Irimajiri was the President
of Honda America Manufacturing
and I'd go up there.
Mr. Irimajiri, or "Mr. Iri," as he was known,
was out on the shop floor every day.
He was President of Honda American Manufacturing,
a large organization,
but every day, he was out on the shop floor.
Now, what was he doing
as he went to the gemba,
where the work gets done, as they say,
or at Honda, they say, "on the spot".
They say,
"The world's greatest experts
are those who are on the spot and have their hands on."
I sat in decision-making meetings at Honda
where a Vice President would offer a suggestion
or a solution to a problem
and another Vice President would say,
"That's interesting, but have you been on the spot?"
and they all, that was code language,
they all knew what that meant.
What it meant is literally, "Had you been out there
on the floor, talking to the people who do the work,"

=== Discipline - Justice, Discipline and Self-Control ===


--- Research Results from Microsoft - Effects on Collaboration ---

(bright music)
-: In the previous lecture,
we talked about the reality of what happened
during the COVID pandemic
and how people like spending time at home,
and they like the whole idea of a hybrid or remote work.
But anytime there's a major change in a system,
and that was a major change in a system, for sure,
while there may be benefits,
there may also be some downsides.
And I want to talk a little bit
about what may be some of those downsides
and what we as managers need to do about it
'cause this is gonna be part of your job as a manager.
Now Microsoft, Microsoft studied during the pandemic,
and they have very good researchers there.
They're very good with statistics.
They studied 61,000 workers working suddenly remotely
rather than in the office,
and they studied what happened
with their communication patterns
and their relationships and so forth.
And I'll attach the results of that research
to this lecture, and you can study it all you want.
But let me just summarize it for you.
First of all, you know, what they found was
that the relationships of those workers became more siloed.
What do I mean by siloed?
Within your team, within your family,
within your formal organizational structure
rather than relationships that are broader,
maybe more serendipitous, outside the formal organization
or that cut across the formal organization.
And that's sort of understandable
'cause you weren't, like,
walking around the office meeting people
or serendipitously, you know, meeting people
in the cafeteria or things like that.
And you may say, "Well, you know, so what?
what does that matter?"
Well, it does matter
because the nature of those relationships
were more asynchronous rather than synchronous.
Synchronous is something that happens at the same time.
My relationship with you is asynchronous.
You may watch this video, you know,
a year from now or anytime you want,
not at the same time.
Synchronous means we're talking together
at the same time, right?
Well, there was less of that
and more email communications, right?
And what Microsoft found was
that reduced the ability of creative communication,
spontaneous communication, across groups.
Research has shown that the strength of relationships
within an organization
and both within the formal organizational structure
of your team
but also relationships that cut across,
which are often spontaneous, serendipitous, right?
Those are an asset to the corporation.
Those relationships result in problem solving.
They result in creativity.
And those were diminished,
therefore diminishing an important asset
of the organization.
This quote sort of sums it up.
"Our results suggest that shifting
to firm-wide remote work caused the collaboration network
to become more heavily siloed with fewer ties
that cut across formal business units
or bridge social structures."
And that results in a lack of
or a decrease in creative activity.
Now, just the idea of social capital,
let me just, you know, introduce that term,
and it's associated with economic activity.
It really is a form of wealth, a form of capital.
And there is social capital within a family, let's just say,
or within a community.
And then there's bridging social capital.
You know, for example, you may have high trust,
which is social capital,
within the manufacturing department,
but then there's the engineering,
and then there's the sales marketing.
And while you may have high trust
within your manufacturing division, if you will,
there may be poor bridging social capital
between marketing and between engineering.
And that reduces collaboration,
and that reduces the ability to solve problems.
So there needs to be bridging social capital
as well as social capital within the unit.
And those were reduced
during the period of everybody staying at home.
We need to think about how to solve that problem
because that is a real problem.

First of all, over the last century, literally,

between management and employee
to an increasingly adult to adult,
increasingly a self-managing environment
where the individual employee
and teams of employees must take responsibility
for managing their own behavior.

is to help your people manage themselves.

you may be saying to yourself, well, wait a minute, Larry,

I wanna manage my people.
I don't want to turn management over to them.

What's your ultimate job?

Your ultimate job is to cause your children

so good at making their own decisions,

Okay?
You want your children to become increasingly self-managed
and you can sort of view the whole from infancy
to late adolescence as increasing levels of self-management.
Well, in the organization as a team leader,
the more your team members are self-managing,
the easier your job is.
The more they rely on you to make decisions
and to control everything, your life is hell.
So the skill of the manager in a team environment

your team members become increasingly self-managing.

=== Conflict Resolution ===

=== Problem Solving Process Improvement ===


--- Transitions ---

(gentle music)
-: As you think about changing your work system, going
from a, let's say, traditional to a hybrid work system,
I think it's good to have perspective
and we tend as just a natural human foible
that we tend to think about the immediate, you know,
we watch TV and we see what looks like a war
and we think, boy, the world is really going to hell.
All these wars, people are dying.
But the reality is, if you look
at the flow of human history, and Steven Pinker's books
and other books kind of give you data on this,
but we're actually living in probably the most peaceful time
human beings have ever lived in on the whole.
But obviously, there are still problems.
So while there are blips on the graph,
there are transitions that are long-term trends
and I think it's worth-looking at them
in terms of the nature of our work management, if you will.
And I'll just give you two, you know, I love my graphics.
I'll give you just two quick graphics
and hopefully, perspectives.
If you back to the 1900
and you look at the majority of work, the majority
of work was either agriculture work or factory work.
And Henry Ford's factories just sort of epitomizes.
Yeah.
And the job of the manager was to control.
And the assumption was workers didn't know.
Managers did know, managers
and engineers knew they made the decisions.
Workers were hired hands, you know, they were labor.
But what happened, well, a lot of things happened first
with the union movement in the '30s and '40s.
And with the development of psychology
and understanding of human nature more,
and then quality management, you know,
and lean Toyota production system,
we changed our thinking about the nature of the workplace.
We increasingly relied on people working in teams,
increasingly people at the first level making decisions,
solving problems, being more autonomous,
not completely autonomous, but more self-directed,
if you will.
Then controlled by man.
And that trend has been going on.
Now, technical and social things often move together,
you know, I mean, the development of the telephone
even the landline telephone changed, social relationships,
the development of the cell phone, obviously the development
of the camera on the cell phone changed a lot of things.
And the internet, obviously, the internet
and high-speed internet broadband and the development
of remote work tools like Zoom, Microsoft Teams, et cetera,
all enabled different human relationships,
different social dynamics.
So that all was leading up
to the COVID-19 that sort of prepared the way, if you will
for what we were able to do in 2020.
Those things are definitely not going away.
You know, Zoom's not going away.
High-speed internet is not going away.
So we have to appreciate that and understand
how to incorporate those things and those trends.
And they're the trends towards increasing self-control,
increasing self-management rather than control.
And I consider that maturity, if you will.
Now, another, just one of my favorite graphics here.
I've, for many, many years I've had
and I've changed it from time to time,
but I've had sort of an old way and a new way.
I, traditional leader, 21st century leader,
I've called it different things, but again,
they reflect the transitions,
the big transitions that we're going through.
And from control to gaining commitment,
you want the best employees, the best workers.
It's not controlling them,
they don't wanna be controlled, they wanna be committed.
Major, important thing, and something that's not
on here is the transition from a scarcity of jobs
to a scarcity of competent labor, workers,
knowledge workers.
And we've been moving
from making stuff as you can, you know, see on here
to making knowledge to processing information.
That's the big change.
The majority of people
in the developed industrial companies,
the United States, Europe, Japan, now increasingly China,
people don't make stuff.
They don't work, they don't make objects,
they don't work in factories.
They process information, they process knowledge.
So that's a big, big transition that's going on
and that implies changes in how managers work,
instead of telling, asking, bringing out
of the people who work on the team that the manager leads,
bringing out from them their competence, their knowledge,
and creating collective wisdom on the team.
The more you create collective wisdom,
the easier the job of the manager is.
And we're recognizing
that all those things should be taken into account.
And you can just look at the list and go down 'em.

your future team process

Successful communication is related to expectations.
And how we perceive communication
is relative to what we want or what we expect.
In other words, if I'm your manager
and I expect that you will communicate with me
at the end of every day
about how things went that day,
but you don't communicate with me 'til the end of the week,
I'm nervous, okay?
You're not meeting my expectation.
You may say my expectation's unreasonable.
That may be right, but it doesn't matter.
It's my expectation, and if you're not conforming
or matching my expectation, I'm uncomfortable, right?
And we don't wanna make our boss anxious or uncomfortable
(laughing) as rule number one, right?
Make your boss comfortable.
So matching expectations.
So be sure that you clarify with your manager
and with your team, the people who report to you,
what their expectations are or set those expectations
in the case of your team.
And then people will be satisfied
if you're conforming to those expectations.
Now, before we get into the specifics here,
I want you to think about a model of what you do
and who you do it with.
You have a team, right?
And a team has a process.
And there's stuff that comes in to the work of your team
that enables the work of your team.
It's easiest to think about in a manufacturing setting,
where chemicals, wire, metals, plastics,
you know, stuff, right,
comes into the plant at one loading dock.
And then you bend it, you melt it, you shape it,
you attach it, you do (laughing) stuff to it, right?
There's a process that adds value.
And then there's output, right, the product goes out
the other loading dock or the other door, if you will.
Maybe you're working on a maternity ward in a hospital.
And even in a health care case like that,
you have a team on the maternity ward,
a team of nurses and doctors.
And there's stuff that comes in.
There is medicine and bandages and people,
you know, patients.
And then you do stuff with them.
You have a process.
And there is output from that process.
So this model, when we can call it a SIPOC,
supply, supplier, input from the supplier,
a process that adds value to the input,
output, and then it goes to a customer, right?
There's an inputting system.
This is basic systems model.
Every system has these properties.
There's input and there's output
and then there's a process in between that does something
to add value to the input that results in the output.
The performance of almost every system is related
to the nature of the feedback from one system to another.
In order words, from your customers, your receiving unit,
to you drives your behavior.
It affects your behavior.
It should affect your behavior, right?

Who gives them feedback,

The law of feedback is very simple.

--- Delegation and Assigning Tasks ---

(soothing music)
-: Hi again, folks.
One of the things that will be a major factor

do it in a way where they feel it's something

They're gonna want to achieve it.

'Cause the higher functioning your team,

Life is much easier.
They accept tasks and delegation
and responsibility much more easily.

they're going to accept responsibility
for completing tasks related to achieving high performance.
And that's what you want.
Score keeping, when the team knows the score,
they know where they are, they know where they're going.
They have all that sense of it's their game.
They're playing the game.
You know, if you have a team playing in the World Series

if they don't know what the score is,
they don't know where they're going.
So again, all these things are linked together.
Do they know the priorities?

So you should be giving them,
telling them what the priority is.
People accept responsibility when a task
or a project is well-defined.
So it's your job as the manager to define tasks
and define responsibilities well,
that then enable an employee or a team member
to take on that task and feel good about it.
When they're highly skilled, you're assignment
of responsibility is gonna be much more successful.
When you provide immediate feedback.
Now, when you assign a project, and you know
there are things that are due at this hour

But it's very important, when you assign a project

so to speak, relatively quick feedback

Not that it's completed, not that they've done it perfectly,

So think about that when you assign a task to somebody,
when do they need feedback,
and when should you ask how it's going
so you can give them feedback
and they know they're on the right track?
So if you look at the list of when delegation is easy
and when delegation is difficult,
they're basically the opposite of those.
Now here's a checklist for delegation.
And you know, you might, the next time
you're delegating a task, you might print
this out and have it in front of you,
or on your computer and just think whether you're doing
each of these things, or how you're doing them,
and it may help you delegate well.
First, define the objectives or the outcome
related to that task or assignment.
You know, we all like to know we're doing
something worthwhile, and we don't like
to waste our time doing something worthless.
So let 'em know, let 'em know why
what you're giving them to do is important.
Why the outcome is important, who it will affect.
Define the tasks of a project,
or define exactly what you want them to do if you know.
Now sometimes you say I'd like you to go
get all the information you can on this subject.
Go study this subject,
and let me know what you think we should do.
Now, when you give an assignment like that,
you don't really know what all, where to look necessarily.
So you're trusting that person to define the tasks themself.
Where do they go look to acquire
the knowledge you're asking 'em to acquire.
But when you do know exactly
what you want them to do, then tell them.
Think about what resources are required.
Is a budget required?
Or are other people required?
Do they need to employ other people in achieving that task?
Think about the resources.
Tell them what the resources are.
They may ask you questions about the resources,
and that's fine, but be sure to define what resources.
what are the boundaries of this task?
For many assignments,
there are areas you don't want them to go.
You have to use the equipment we have to get this done.
You can't buy new equipment.
That's a boundary.
We can change some elements of the software
to get this task done, but we can't
go out and buy new software to get this done.
Well, those are boundary statements, right?
So if you're assigning a task to me,
I'd like to know where the boundaries are.

And you may tell them, you know,
if you're not sure where the boundaries are,
what you can and can't do, please come

But boundary definition is important.
State the desired result,
particularly if it's a result that can be measured.
You know, if it's improvement in output by X amount
or improvement in quality by X amount,
be very clear about that.
When is the project or the task to be completed?
On almost every assignment, there's a timeframe,
unless it's just an ongoing job.
But for most, when you're delegating
tasks or projects, what is the timeline.
Be very clear about that.
And think about when you're gonna get feedback from them.
Don't wait until the day something is due.
If it's something that should require a month of work,
don't wait until the last day to ask how it's going.
Ask how it's going 10 and 20 days in,
and ask them if it's gonna
be ready by the end of that month,
if that's the timeline, right.
So make sure they're on the timeline
and they're working towards that timeline.
But have a timeline.
To whom are they reporting?
Are they just reporting to you
or are they reporting to a team?
Or are they reporting to somebody above you perhaps?
That could even be.
But to whom are they reporting?
And when do you expect that report?
You know, in all good communication,
you check out with the other person whether they understand.
So when you go through the previous eight items
on this list with your subordinate or with your team,
ask them if they have the same understanding.
Ask them if it's clear to them.
And if it's not, then of course you clarify.
So those are just some guidelines for assigning tasks

--- Improving Human Performance ---

(gentle music)
-: Hi again, friends.
During this section of the course,
we're going to get into the whole issue of managing
and improving the performance of people.
And you'll remember my three circles,
which you'll see too often, I'm sure.
We start with the people area and talk about managing,
developing, and leading people,
and then we'll talk about teams,
and then we'll talk about process.
But there are several, there's a big section on people,
but it's broken down into several sections.
The first section here, I want to talk
about developing skills, developing competence.
Now, if you back up and you think about human performance,
what is it that makes up human performance?
And that's a complex subject,
but the basics of it are really pretty simple.
And think about it this way.
On the one hand, there's the ability to do something,
the skill, the competence,
and then there's the motivation to do something.
In other words, if I have never learned how to touch type
and you put pages in front of me and a keyboard
and you say, "Larry, I'll give you a million dollars
"if you can type those words at 70 words a minute."
Now, not that I'm a money grubbing capitalist,
but I'll take the million dollars.
I'd like to be able to do it.
Now, if I've never learned to touch type, can I do it?
I'm certainly motivated, right?
But I don't have the skill. I can't do it.
If you say,
"Larry, I'll give you a million dollars to sing an opera,"
it ain't gonna happen
'cause Larry Miller cannot sing an opera,
and there are many, many other things that I cannot do.
And it's not wanting to. It's not motivation.
It's simply lacking the skill.
So we have to have the skill to perform,
the competency to perform.
Plus, we have to have the motivation.
We have to have both of those things.
So in this section of the course,
I want to talk about developing competency,
the can do versus the will or won't do motivation part.
So, let's start breaking that down,
and here's, I'm gonna get out of the way
so you can see this diagram.
A long time ago, probably 30 years ago, I don't know,
I read a book by Robert Mager and Peter Pipe
called "Managing Human Performance."
I think that was the title of it.
And this is that I've used this for years
and I've stolen it from Robert Mager.
I simply call it the Mager Model.
And the Mager Model very simply
says you define what the behavior is,
what the performance you want,
and then there is the can do versus the won't do.
There's the ability to do it versus the won't do.
And then there are on each side,
and we'll talk about the motivation in another section
after this one of the course,
but I want to talk about the can'ts or can do
in this section.
And when you look at, when somebody can't do something,
they're not able to do it,
whether it's playing a musical instrument or a sport
or touch typing or operating a piece of equipment
in your organization or writing a certain kind of code,
if they can't do it, what do you do?
And to break that down, think about it this way.
There's behavioral skills.
Like, I play the guitar, and if you play the guitar,
there are patterns that become habitual,
become very automatic.
I do finger style, blues kind of guitar,
and the thumb, you know, bum-bum, bum-bum, bum-bum,
it's the rhythm section of the guitar, right?
And you learn to do that.
You don't think, you know,
it just bum-bum, bum-bum, does that automatically
and that's your rhythm,
and then you add the melody with the fingers.
And there are patterns on the left hand
and patterns on the right hand,
and as you learn to play the guitar,
initially you struggle with those patterns.
Initially have to go remember, "Oh yeah, it goes here,
"then it goes there, then this goes there.
"There's a C chord and a G, and where's that?"
And your fingers don't really work well, you know?
But once you have developed some mastery of the skill,
it becomes very automatic.
You just change chords really almost without thinking,
and the particularly the right hand patterns
are very, very automatic for you
and that's a sign that you've developed the skill.
You know, when we drive a car, for example,
we don't really think about what we're doing, do we?
I mean, we don't think, "Okay, now I'm gonna go right,
"so let's see, I turn the wheel which way?
"Oh yeah, I turn the wheel this way."
No, you don't think that. You just do it, right?
It's very automatic
because you've developed the habit patterns,
you've developed the skill.
All skills are things that become habitual for you, right?
If they're not habitual,
you really haven't developed the skill.
Now, if you're gonna develop a skill,
and skills are different than knowledge, right?
You know, you can read a book and develop knowledge,
but you can't read a book and develop the skill.
There's education and there's training.
This course is both education and training.
I think the training is very important,
and that means you have to practice things.
That's why I give you assignments
and ask you to work with a coach,
because that's how you're going to develop skills.
You're gonna develop the habits.
So if we wanna develop a skill, we have to provide training.
And I'm gonna give you a model in the next lecture
for providing very basic training.
You as a manager, and there's just no way to escape this,
if you think about the coach of a baseball team
or any athletic team, you know, yeah, they give direction
and they say who goes into play now
and who comes out now, and what plays are we gonna run.
But really the most important thing they do
is help their players develop competence,
help them develop skill.
Coaching a team of unskilled players is hell.
Coaching a team of highly skilled players is a pleasure.
Well, you want a team of highly skilled players,
and you cannot simply rely on the training department.
You know, it's not okay to say, "Well, that's not my job.
"That's the training department's job."
No, the training department may provide certain skills,
but you as the manager who is on the spot,
observing what people are doing,
you are in the best position to help people develop skills.
So in the next lecture,
let me give you a model for doing that.
Thanks.
(uplifting music)

--- A Model for Basic Skills Training ---

(gentle music)
-: Hey again, friends.
I want to give you a basic, simple,
but well proven model for developing employees' skills.
And I know you may think
it's the training department's problem, but no, it's not.
It's your team, your employees. It's your problem.
And being on the spot, being able to observe them,
you really are in the best position
to do the most fundamental training
in basic skills on the job.
Now, here's a six-step model,
and it in part comes from something
called Training Within the Industry, TWI,
which was developed during World War II
to train new employees in airplane factories
and so forth very quickly 'cause there wasn't a lot of time
and they had to hire a lot of people
who'd never been to work,
a lot of women, for example,
who had never been in the workplace
and they had to develop their skills very quickly.
This model worked, and after the war was over,
when American troops and development people went to Japan,
they trained Japanese industry
in the Training Within Industry model,
and to this day, it is still used in Toyota
and in other companies.
So, it's proven to work very well.
Now, I've modified it a little bit
based on my own experience, but that's unimportant.
Here's a six-step model,
and let's go through each of the steps.
Now, a skill is a set of behaviors, right?
You know, if you watch somebody play the guitar
and it looks like one thing.
(Larry hums)
And it sounds good, but it's not one thing.
It's 10 different things going on, right?
There's the position of the hand.
There's the finger. Where do the fingers go?
There's the motion in the right hand,
assuming they're right-handed.
And each of those specific behaviors
is I'll call a pinpointed behavior.
A pinpointed behavior is a behavior
that two people can observe independently
and see the same thing, right?
And that's what we wanna do.
We wanna pinpoint the behaviors
because they're easy to describe to somebody, right?
When you describe a whole skill,
it's like too much, it's too complex,
but if you break it down,
and I'm gonna ask you to do this in an assignment.
I'm ask you to take a skill
that's important in your work area
and I want you to break it down into its component parts,
its component pinpointed behaviors,
and then I want you to train somebody in that skill
or those pinpointed behaviors.
Now, the first thing you do is you define the skill
and you break it down into the pinpointed behaviors.
And in any skill, whether it's a sport,
shooting the ball through the hoop in basketball
or almost any other skill, dancing, you know, perhaps,
are all skills that are comprised
of a specific pinpointed behavior.
So, identify your skill,
and then I want you to break it down
into the three, five, 10 specific pinpointed behaviors
that will make it digestible.
You know, one of the principles here
is the principle of shaping behavior.
Shaping behavior says, you don't say, "Learn French.
"Come tell me when you've finished doing that."
No, no, no.
You say, "Here are three verbs and here are three nouns.
"Learn those," right?
And then you go on to the next three and then the next 10.
And so you build on the first pinpointed behaviors.
You build the next pinpointed behaviors,
until the whole skill gets developed.
But first, break it down.
Prepare the student. What does prepare the student mean?
Make sure they're in the right place.
Sometimes there's equipment required,
a sitting position required, the tools required.
And tell them why it's important.
You know, they need motivation to learn a skill.
So why do you care? Why should they care?
What does it matter to the team?
What does it matter to the company?
You know, give them the answers to those things
so they have some motivation to do it.
Then present the skill.
And by presenting the skill, I mean modeling it, right?
Now, if you wanna learn to play the guitar,
go on YouTube and find,
there's lots of instructional videos on YouTube.
Find a tune that you like
and watch somebody who plays it really well.
And watch how they have their hands, watch how they go.
You know, listen to it. Get it in your mind.
And then begin to break down just one section,
one small section, into the pinpointed behaviors.
So similarly, whatever the skill is at work,
give your student, your employee, the opportunity
to observe somebody who's good at,
and it may not even be you.
It may be some other employee, right? And that's fine.
Have them observe the skill.
Point out to them what the behaviors are.
What is the person doing that makes them competent,
that makes them good.
So, we learn a lot by observation,
by seeing somebody else do it,
so give them that opportunity.
Then practice the pinpointed behaviors.
Not the whole chain of behaviors.
Not 10 different behaviors or the whole skill,
but baby steps, right?
I mean, practice just a few of the behaviors
repeatedly, right?
Watch them, observe them, give them feedback.
Tell them, "That's great,"
or tell them, "Well, if you moved your hand a little here,
"a little there," or whatever the difference is
between competently performing that behavior
and the way they're doing it,
you know, give them feedback on that.
Then watch them practice again.
Watch them practice repeatedly.
Once that becomes fluid, once that becomes easy for them,
they've got the habit down, then you go on to some more
pinpointed behaviors in the sequence,
and you repeat that over and over again
until they've got the whole chain of behaviors.
In behavioral psychology,
the idea of pinpointing is one concept
and the idea of chaining is another concept,
where you take those behaviors
and now you start practicing them together in sequence
'til you've got the whole chain of behaviors,
if that makes sense?
Observe them now practicing the entire chain,
the entire skill.
Praise them, assuming they're doing it well now.
Give them positive feedback or give them some coaching,
some advice as to how they might do it better and improve.
And then they will become comfortable
and they will become confident as they have your praise.
Let them then do their job.
Let them then practice that skill, and follow up.
You know, come back in a day or in a week and observe again.
See that they're doing it well
'cause they might've forgotten some component of it.
But follow up in time, and again, give them feedback,
and then they will have the skill.
Does that make sense?
You know, the way we develop,
this is how human beings develop, right?
This is how children develop
from crawling to walking to running.
They go through that chaining behavior,
learning pinpointed behaviors.
And in an organization,
we improve the organization this way.
There's a big goal, a big challenge, right?
You know, how I want to be able
to play the musical instrument, you know?
I want to be able to play like Chet Atkins or something.
Good luck.
But then we practice the pinpointed behaviors
as we go up the mountain, right?
As we get better and better.
And in an organization,
that improves the measurable performance
as we improve skills, right?
So we don't just go after measurable performance.
We have to build the skills of our employees.
Then we'll get the measurable performance.
I hope that makes sense.
I want to come back and I want to give you an example
from my early career in prison.
Thanks.
(uplifting music)

--- An Example of Skill Training ---

(calm upbeat music)
-: Hi again.
After college, my very first job was in a prison in Raleigh,
North Carolina, working as a counselor.
And I set up a whole systemic change in the organization,
in the prison, where it was a behavior modification effort,
where the idea was inmates would learn to earn.
And they worked in a license tag plant in a printing shop
and they lived in four dormitories,
and we made one dormitory a luxury dorm,
one a standard dorm, one a luxury,
quality, standard, and efficiency dorm.
And we charged rent.
We paid them in points for the work they did
and then they paid rent on the dormitories,
obviously the luxury dorm charged the highest rent
and so forth.
Now, as part of that effort, I observed my inmates
and I learned something about them.
And one thing I learned about them was
there were just some fundamental, I mean,
very fundamental skills they did not have.
You know, the families they came from,
if they were families at all,
they didn't provide the skills.
The skills that you and I might take for granted,
you cannot take for granted in these young inmates I had.
And one of the skills that they lacked
was how to interview for a job.
And I set up a role playing thing
where I was in an office, I had a desk,
and I had a video camera, one of the early video cameras.
And I would bring John, let's call him, into the office
and I say, "John, you know, when you get out,
do you wanna get a job?"
They'd think, "Yeah."
They knew that was the right answer, okay?
They'd say yes.
And I say, "Well, you're probably gonna have to
go interview for a job then, aren't you?"
And they go, "I guess so."
"Have you ever done that?"
"No," and I say, "You know, if you can do it well,
you're more likely to get the job
than if you can't do it well or you do it poorly.
Does that make sense?"
They'd say, "Yeah, that makes sense."
And I'd say, "Well, why don't we practice?
You can't get in trouble here,
you can't lose a job here, you know?
But we can really practice without any fear
of anything going wrong."
And he said, "Okay."
And I said, "Well, you go out the door of the office,
and I want you to knock on the door
and I want you to come in.
And I'm the personnel manager in a mobile home factory,
and we have jobs for guys to hammer nails
and help with the construction of a mobile home.
Does that make sense?"
He'd say, "Yeah."
"So I'm gonna turn the video camera,
and let's watch what happens, okay?
I'll show it back to you, and you can see what happens."
So they go outside the office and I'd say,
"Okay, knock on the door."
And I'd hear, boom.
Just one boom.
Could hardly hear it.
I'd say, "John, come on in."
And they'd open the door.
These guys didn't trust anybody.
They'd open the door and they'd kinda look around.
And I'd say, "Come on in."
They open the door.
And I don't know what it was.
The guys, they walk like this.
And they bopped.
They usually had their shirt hanging out.
And they didn't walk in a straight line.
They sort of snaked, you know?
And they'd kind of go...
Walking towards me.
And then he'd come in front of me
and I'd stick my hand out, and he'd look at my hand
like he'd never seen it before, right?
I'd say, "Hi, John."
And they'd go like this, you know?
You know the phrase close to your vest?
They'd keep their hand close to their vest, close like this.
And I'd say, "Sit down, have a seat."
And they'd sit down, and he put his elbows on my desk,
and he was looking around, and they were still bopping.
They had rhythm, they had music in their brain,
and they were bopping.
And I'd ask him a question or two, and...
Then he'd look.
Stop, stop.
"Let's watch that."
And I'd show them the video back and they'd watch it.
And I'd say, "John, if you were hiring somebody,
would you hire this guy?"
And you know what he said?
He said, "I don't think so."
And I said, "Why?"
He said, "He doesn't look like he wants the job."
I said, "Good observation.
Why do you think he doesn't want the job?"
And he'd say, "I don't know,
he just doesn't look like he wants the job."
And I'd say, "Well, let's..."
What I'm thinking is pinpoint behavior.
"Let's break down what he's doing,
and let's see if we can figure out
how to look like you want the job.
Right, does that make sense?"
And I said, "You know, when you shake hands,
let's just take that, you kinda look like this, right?
Look at my arm.
You see this?
Grab my hand."
And he'd...
"No, no, no, I said.
Stick your hand out like this.
Look at my arm, look.
So you stick it out.
There you go, good.
Give me a good shake, you're a strong guy."
These guys were athletes, you know?
They didn't lack any physical ability.
Shake my hand, good, nice and firm.
Would shake it for a minute, that's good,
good, good, good, good.
Okay, that's one thing.
"You know, just one other thing you might try.
When you knock on the door, instead of just one little...
Why don't you go bump, bump, or bump, bump, bump, you know?
I think people who are more confident go bump, bump, bump.
So why don't we try it again?"
Okay, go out.
They can knock on the door, right?
Funny thing happens.
He knocks on the door, you know what he does now?
He opens the door more confidently.
Maybe, or maybe not, now he walks in a straight line.
He sticks out his hand, right?
And we maybe go through this three,
four, or five times, right?
Each time, he's learning a pinpointed behavior,
practicing that behavior, watching himself,
which is the best feedback of, all right?
I would point something out, or model,
I'm modeling for him. how to shake hands, right?
I'm telling you, guaranteed, within one hour,
I can have that inmate who has never interviewed for a job,
never been taught how to shake hands,
I can have him knocking on the door,
walking in a straight line, looking me in the eye,
which they didn't do.
Looking me in the eye, shaking my hand,
sitting down at the desk and sitting upright.
Not with his elbows on the table,
not wandering around the room bopping, right?
But looking at me.
Now, after an hour, when I play it back for him, I say,
"Would you hire this guy now?"
He says, "Yeah, maybe I would hire him."
Good.
Now, you know, I never talked psychology to him.
I never talked about his ego, and his ID,
and his self-esteem, and his confidence.
But is he more confident now?
Of course he's more confident now.
You know why?
Because he's right.
Because in fact, now he knows what he's doing.
Now he has a clue what to do, and he can do it.
He can see that he can do it.
And now he's developing the chain of behaviors.
He's putting this together and it's both changing, in fact,
his probability of getting the job when he interviews,
but it's even changing the probability
that he will show up to the interview.
'Cause inmates often don't show up for interviews
because they're scared.
They're scared, they have no confidence.
And you know why they're scared?
'Cause they're right.
Because they know they don't know what they're doing.
But once you teach them and they know what to do,
they'll do it and they'll show up for the job, right?
Does that make sense?
So their internal things are changing,
at the same time, you're changing external behavior.
Those things are very, very related.
And this is not nothing rocket science here is it?
But you know what, for an inmate,
getting out of prison, it's a big, big deal.
It's an absolutely core skill.
And unfortunately in the United States, we don't do it.
You know, we're terrible at this.
Then we wonder why they come back.
Well, they come back because they don't have the skills,
that's why they come back.
Well, anyway, I use that just as an example,
and if you think about those six steps
of developing a skill of training, they're all there.
They're all there in that example.
You think about the total skill of interviewing.
You break it down into pinpoint the behaviors.
You observe them do it, right?
You model for them.
"Look at my arm."
You know, you model the behavior.
You have them practice, "Come on, shake.
Come on shake my hand."
They practice the behavior, they watch themselves back,
they get feedback.
Then they put the whole chain together
once they've learned the 10 skills or the 10 behaviors,
whatever the number is.
And then they gain confidence,
and then in the employee team setting,
in your work setting, they are better employees,
and your job as a manager becomes a lot easier.
Hope all that makes sense.
Thanks a lot folks.

--- The Hierarchy of Motivation ---

(gentle music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let's switch now from
the competency side of the equation,
to the motivation side of the equation.
You'll remember my little formula here,
of competency times motivation equals performance.
Which is pretty right,
I mean, there are other factors,
but this is a nice simple way of thinking
about what results in human performance on your team,
or in your organization.
And you know, the subject of motivation,
and I'm sure you've thought about this,
I'm sure you've thought about it
in terms of your own motivation from time to time.
And you've probably thought about it in terms
of the motivation of others around you,
or your team members.
There have been hundreds of books
written on the subject of motivation.
I've written a few of them.
And there are numerous theories of motivation,
some named after the name of some professor
who came up with an interesting construct.
I'm gonna give you a simple model.
It's not a, it's not a dumb model,
it's a smart model, but it's simple.
And what I've attempted to do in this model,
is integrate, or unify different theories of motivation,
with a particular eye towards thinking about
what you can actually do as a manager.
You know, there's some theories of motivation
that go into psychological states,
and you know, even genetics,
which you can't do anything about.
So it's kind of none of your business.
You don't have to worry about it.
Just put that outta your mind.
You, as a manager have to think about,
how do I affect the motivation of those around me,
and those under me, my employees?
So, this model addresses things
that you can do something about, which is your job.
And your job, you know,
there are theories of motivation that say,
"Well, money doesn't matter.
People don't care about money.
They just care about self-esteem or something else."
Look, don't worry about that.
For you, it's not important.
What your job is, is to maximize all the potential sources
of motivation that can impact your people.
So, you know, human beings are different.
You know, we're diverse, and we're unique,
and depending upon how we were raised,
and what our interests are, you know,
some people are very interested in money,
and moving up the ladder.
Others are more interested in family time.
There are lots of different interests that people have.
So you can't, you know, you're not smart enough,
and neither am I, to say,
"Well, I'm gonna do this for this person,
and I'm gonna do that for that person,
I'm gonna do this for this person."
Because you have to deal with them differently.
No, you don't.
What you have to do,
is you have to optimize
all the potential sources of motivation
for your team members.
And if you're doing that,
some people will respond to some things,
and others will respond to other things,
and you'll be doing your job.
Now, here's my little model.
I'll call it a hierarchy of motivation.
And I think you can, if you think about your own life,
I think you can see this, and understand it.
And in the following lectures,
I'll go into more depth into each of these areas.
But one way of thinking about motivation,
is to think about this pyramid,
where at the top of the pyramid,
there, we have a spiritual nature.
We have a search for meaning in our life,
a search for purpose in our life.
And that's the big deal for human beings,
for the human condition.
It's why we seek out religions,
and why all religions all over the world,
give purpose and meaning to their followers.
And people follow them precisely for that reason,
because they need meaning,
and purpose in their life.
And we don't have to say,
"Well, one religion is better than another,"
or right or wrong, or any of that.
None of that matters.
I'm just sort of stating a sociological fact,
that religion and purpose,
and that purpose may be country,
it may be family, it may be community,
in addition to religion,
but we seek that meaning.
Now, is there meaning at work?
Is there purposefulness at work?
And I would say, "Of course there is."
Which doesn't mean it's quote, "religious,"
but we find ways of finding meaning in our work.
I find meaning in my work.
I hope you find meaning in your work.
So I'll call this sort of the spiritual realm,
or the realm of purpose.
You can think of that as the higher level of motivation.
Then there's how we interact with other people.
There's community, there's teams,
there's families, there's social bonding.
And it is our nature,
it is in the human genetic code, if you will,
in the human nature to be motivated
by being part of a group.
You know, when I, when I worked in prisons,
people would say there's a gang problem.
And I tell 'em, "No, there's not a gang problem.
Gangs are the solution to the problem."
You know what the real problem is?
You show me a community where
the family unit is healthy and strong,
I'll show you a community
that does not have a gang problem.
Show me a community where family units
are in disintegration,
where there is no safety
for the young person in the family.
And what do they seek?
They seek a group, right?
And that group is in the inner city, and other places.
And it happens all around the world, by the way.
That's the gang.
That's the group that's meeting
the psychological need of bonding.
Well, in the workplace.
And I'm gonna talk a lot about teams
as we go through the course.
But we like to work in groups,
we like to work in teams.
And I'll come back and talk about that.
But that's the social dimension of motivation.
And it's a major driver in our workplace.
Then there's what I'll call situational motivation.
And that's, that's our immediate environment.
That's the consequences to our behavior.
That's the prompts, the stoplight,
the sign that says, "Safety."
"Wear a safety hat," you know,
"Wear a hard hat," or "Wear safety gloves,"
or "Wear safety glasses,"
or, you know, we see these things
in our work environment,
that like, the red light, the traffic light,
the stop sign, they're meant to be cues, prompts,
to stimulate a certain behavior, right?
And then there are consequences to behavior.
And we'll talk more about that in some depth.
But those are things that we do manage.
You know. We manage the social more
than we manage the spiritual,
although we do have an effect on that.
And even more so,
we manage the situational impact on motivation.
So, we're gonna talk about how to do that.
And you can think of that
as sort of going from the material to the spiritual,
and by, you know, saying that's material.
I don't mean that in a negative way,
it's just more immediate.
It's more the, you know, the more,
the immediate consequences to our behavior.
So, we're gonna go through that model
in the following lectures,
and I hope you can see things,
and I'm sure you can see things
that you will be able to do,
to maximize the motivation of your people.
Thanks.
(gentle music)
(upbeat music)

God's own mad lover should die for the kiss

and panic and then you should run run for the hills get out.

about where your company is.
It's not, you know, the immediate situational motivation
that you can do a lot about,
but I do think it's a worthwhile insight into motivation
in your organization.
I hope that helps, thanks.

--- Situational Motivation ---

(gentle music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let's go to the bottom of that pyramid
and let's talk about situational motivation.
And these are the influences on human behavior or motivation
that you are most likely to be able to affect or manipulate
or change because they're in your environment
and you have a pretty good degree of control
over your environment and the things
that impact the behavior of your employees.
So it's a good model to study and analyze human behavior.
In fact, it's called behavior analysis,
is the field of psychology that this is drawn from.
And it's about behavior.
Remember our discussion about pinpointed behavior
when we talked about developing skills.
That you don't focus on attitudes or character traits,
you focus on observable behavior.
And behavior analysis is based on that idea also.
And it's the basis of all training
whether your training, you know, a child to engage in,
you know, to clean their room, if you will.
Or if you're training a dog to sit and roll over,
whatever, or a horse, or if you're training human beings
in really any environment.
Anytime you wanna change their behavior,
affect their behavior,
it's desirable to focus on pinpointed behavior.
Now, this simple diagram
says they're antecedents and consequences.
It's the ABC model, antecedent, behavior, consequence.
Very easy to to remember.
And what, you know, what's an antecedent?
An antecedent is something that comes before the behavior
that impacts the behavior.
A consequence is something that comes after the behavior
that serves to reinforce, strengthen the behavior
or it may be a punishment that reduces the behavior
or maybe a neutral consequence,
which really means no consequence
and that turns to extinguish,
tends to extinguish the behavior,
reduce the frequency of the behavior.
Now let's just talk about antecedents for the moment.
What is an antecedent?
The easiest way to think about antecedent,
and they're in our environment all the time,
the clock on the wall is an antecedent
to go to lunch or stop work or begin work.
The traffic light, red light, green light, yellow light,
those are antecedents.
They're, you can think of them as prompts or cues.
That all means the same thing.
But they're something we see, a stimuli,
in our environment that then cues us,
triggers, you can call them triggers if you want also,
that triggers a behavior, right?
Hopefully when you see a red light,
you have learned, you've been taught to respond to that
by taking your foot off the gas
and putting it on the brake.
That triggers that behavior or prompts that behavior.
Either, any of that language is good.
Now, what makes an antecedent work?
Well, why do we stop at a red light?
Well, the reason we stop at a red light
is that we have learned.
And all this is called learning theory, right?
If you want a theory for it, it's simply learning theory.
It's how human beings, in fact all living organisms,
at least all animals or species, how we learn.
We learn from antecedents
and we learn that they're important
because there's a consequence, right?
So like, if you don't stop at a red light,
there are consequences to that behavior.
You may have an accident, which is a severe consequence.
Or you may get a ticket, the police may stop you.
Or now around where I live,
there are these cameras all the time that are, you know,
flash when you, if you speed or you go through a red light
and you get a ticket in the mail.
So there are consequences to behavior.
Now, once you understand this very simple model
it gives you a good clue as to what you can do
to encourage somebody's behavior
or change somebody's behavior.
Let me just give you a couple stories that illustrate.
Very early in my career,

And it had improved attendance,

And there was Mary, and this graph is Mary's graph.

Everybody knew her well, everybody liked her.
But she was slow.
And there was something called

She was always there,

You know, she may know that she's, you know,

'Cause what you're talking about doing now

You know, that 35% efficiency, that worked for her.
I mean, she had a job, she got paid every week.
You were reinforcing that behavior.
So if you're gonna change the consequences,
maybe Mary oughta know that.
Maybe you oughta give her some feedback.
So why don't you?"
And we talked a lot about feedback.
So we said, "Let's just make a graph

Now this was new behavior for the supervisor

now something's gonna change, right?

And Mary, you know, when you ask somebody what they can do,

Mary had gotten to 125% standard operating efficiency

She was able, she was capable of performing,

There was no consequence,
there was no reinforcement for doing better.
There was no antecedent.
Let's think about that whole ABC model.
When you show her the graph and you say,
"You know, here's how you're performing."
That's an antecedent, right?
And you say, "By the way, other people are performing better

That's an antecedent. Setting a goal is an antecedent.
It all comes before the behavior, right?
Now after the behavior, what's the consequence?
And it's not like a big prize, it's not money,
it's just the feedback and the recognition.
You know, the manager saying, "Great job, Mary."
That is a consequence to behavior, and she improved.
Now if you understand this model,
