--- Keys to Effectiveness ---

(light music)
-: I wanna talk about definitions for a minute.
And this may seem boring,
but it's actually very significant.
In the behavior analysis or behavior modification,
behavioral psychology literature,
there are very precise definitions
for positive reinforcement, for example.
You know what positive reinforcement means?
Now, when we think about it, it's positive, right?
It's desirable.
It's good.
Yes.
But the term reinforcement is defined
as a stimulus, right,
a consequence, consequence is a stimulus,
that has the effect of increasing the strength or rate
of the behavior.
In other words, it's an empirical definition.
If behavior does not increase in strength or rate,
there, in fact, is not positive reinforcement.
You can say you praised somebody,
but some people find praise aversive.
They don't like it.
And it doesn't increase their rate of behavior.
So it is therefore not really positive reinforcement.
We have to become scientists.
We have to study the effect of our actions
and the effect of consequences on behavior.
So think about when you praise somebody
or you have some incentive in the workplace,
does it actually work by the data?
Does the data say yes, people are responding to that?
Positive means presentation.
You present something.
Negative reinforcement still increases the rate of behavior.
It's reinforcement, but the modifier
of negative means removing something
that then increases the rate of behavior.
I know it seems strange,
but getting out of the rain and stopping the rain
is negative reinforcement.
It reinforces the behavior of moving
from place A to place B.
And the reinforcer is the termination of the rain
on your body.
So that's actually negative reinforcement.
Punishment, and you think punishment, that's mean,
it's being bad, it's yelling, it's, you know?
No, punishment is a consequence that results
in a reduction in the rate of behavior, right?
Now, sadly, and parents are often terrible at this,
they yell, scream, even spank a child
when the child is really begging for attention.
They need attention.
And even yelling at them
may reinforce the very behavior they're engaged in
'cause they've learned that's a way
to get daddy's attention.
And you may think, "Well, I'm punishing them."
Well, not if it doesn't reduce the rate of that behavior.
And by the way, I am a parent, of course.
My children are all grown.
I have eight grandchildren.
But an important lesson is
that anytime there is a behavior that you wanna reduce,
you know, a child doing something wrong,
it's very important to substitute some other behavior.
If they're gonna stop doing it,
they're gonna do something else, right?
So the question is: What else?
So if you don't want them doing that,
what is the other behavior that you're reinforcing?
Are you reinforcing reading a book, right?
I mean, what's the consequence for reading a book
if you don't want them to watch so much television?
You can say, "Turn off the television,"
but then they sit there and go, "Oh, what do I do now?"
Well, you gotta tell 'em what to do now.
You gotta give 'em something positive to do,
and you need to praise them.
You need to ask them about what they're reading.
You need to talk to them about it.
You need to tell 'em, "That's so interesting."
You know, "I'm really glad that you're reading that."
And maybe even some reward for every book that you read.
I mean, something positive needs to happen
that will then replace that negative behavior
or the behavior you wanna reduce, whatever that is.
It's an important lesson.
Extinction means removing reinforcement
and the behavior will go down,
although very often, the behavior goes up initially
because the reinforcement has maintained that behavior.
And the person initially thinks,
"Well, wait a a minute, it always worked, so I try harder."
You know, this is the example of the child
who you put to bed and screams,
and mommy comes back in and holds them.
Mommy's actually reinforcing the behavior of screaming
when you put them in bed.
Well, if you now you don't go into the room,
they're gonna say, "Hey, what's wrong with mommy?
Mommy always come in when I scream."
So they're gonna scream louder, right?
Well, there's called an extinction curve
were initially it goes up and then it goes down.
They give up, basically, right?
So the behavior will be extinguished,
although it goes through that initial jump in behavior.
Those definitions, you know, really do matter.
So there's one other concept that I'll share with you.
It's called the balance of consequences.
And it's an important understanding,
because you know, we may think that,
you know, well, for most of our behavior,
there are choices, right?
I can stay late and get the job done,
or I can go home and be on time for dinner, for example.
That's a choice.
Now, it's not like everything is positive on one side
and everything is negative on the other.
No, there's a balance of consequences.
In fact, there's a positive consequence
to staying at work and getting the job done.
Hopefully, my boss will think that's great and praise me.
Or I'll look good or whatever the consequence is.
But on the other hand,
there's a positive consequence for getting home.
You know, my spouse will be more happy with me.
My children will be happier with me.
And that's a positive consequence for the other behavior.
So there's this sorta teeter-totter, you know,
where for most of our decisions and most of our behavior,
there are positive and negative consequences on both sides.
Now, you know how a teeter-totter is, right?
Does everything have to shift to one side
to change the behavior?
No, it doesn't.
A slight shift.
If you have 50 pounds on this side
and 50 pounds on this side,
and it's right even, well, a shift of one pound
changes the whole thing, doesn't it?
So when we think about reinforcing behavior,
we don't have to make everything positive,
you know, on one side.
We have to realize that even a slight shift,
a slight change in the consequences
can dramatically change the performance.
One other concept I wanna share,
and this is an important one.
Four to one.
I was reading Jeff Liker's book on Toyota culture.
And on page 400 and something,
forget what, he talks about Toyota practicing four to one.
Four positives to one negative.
Now, my bet is that hardly anybody at Toyota, if anybody,
knows where this comes from, but I do.
And it comes from back in the 1970s
when I was at Behavioral Systems in Atlanta, Georgia,
and I still have a little paperweight
that says four to one on it.
There was some research done by a psychologist
named Ogden Lindsley in classrooms.
And Ogden Lindsley was trying to find
the optimum rate of positive to negative comments
by teachers to students
that optimized the performance of students.
Don't ask me for the details of how he did this.
But what his research said in the conclusion
was that the optimum ratio of positive to negative,
it's worth thinking about this in terms
of how you interact with your spouse
or how you interact with your children
or with your employees,
but he found that the optimum ratio
of positive to negative comments was 3.57 to one.
This is a bit of trivia you can really impress
your friends (laughing) with.
3.57 to one was the optimum ratio
that maximized the performance of children.
Now, does this generalized to adults?
I have no idea.
But we took that and we averaged it to four.
So we made it instead of 3.57 (laughing),
we made it four to one.
And this became sort of a game,
that it was a rule where we actually had managers
having four to one cards in their pocket,
where every time they interacted with an employee,
they'd mark down that it was either positive or negative.
You could try this yourself.
And it's sort of interesting just to see:
Are you more negative or positive?
When you approach an employee in the work setting,
are they more likely to think
that you're gonna have something positive to say
or something negative to say to them?
Are you more likely to criticize them?
Are you more likely to find fault with them?
Or are you more likely to recognize the good things
that they're doing?
Well, optimum performance comes
from 3.57 (laughing) to one or four to one.
But weighted on that side of the scale
in terms of positive to negative comments to employees.
So you know, this has gone all the way,
you know, I didn't see this thing at Toyota until recently.
But over a 40-year period,
this thing has migrated, this four to one principle,
from when we started using it in textile mills
back in the 1970s.
And it's a valid idea.
And it's just, you know, a game you can play
to check yourself about whether you're behaving in a way
that optimizes the motivation of your people.
Let's come back and I'd like to talk about intrinsic
and extrinsic motivation.
It's a major subject of some contention
within the psychology community.
I'll come back and talk about it.

--- Keys to Intrinsic Motivation ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi folks.
There's another concept regarding positive reinforcement
or motivation that I'd like to share with you.
And it may seem a little academic at first,
but I don't wanna be academic.
I wanna make it very practical for you
but it is worth understanding

Now, just, you know, as background,
there are various ways of defining motivation.
One is intrinsic meaning
from the work itself or the activity itself.
Like for some playing a musical instrument
they don't need to be rewarded by anybody.
There doesn't need to be some outside positive

I enjoy playing the guitar.
I don't ever perform, you know,
in front of other people unless I have to.
I certainly don't strive for that.
I like the activity itself and I like learning a new tune.
And just enjoy it intrinsically.
It would be great if all work
were so intrinsically reinforcing.
There was no need for any extrinsic reinforcement
like compensation or any of those sorts of things,
but it ain't true.
We need extrinsic reinforcement.
Another way to sort of divide types of reinforcement
is tangible and social or intangible.
Tangible or you know, money is tangible.
You can do something with it.
Any object, you know, a plaque,
an award as a tangible thing.
But for many of those things,
like an award,
there's also a social component that goes along with it.
There's the approval of other people,
that's social.
And then there's immediate
and long-term reinforcement things that happen right away.
And then, you know, we would all like our children
to study every night as you know,
seventh graders.
Because it will help them to get into college
a good college and have a good career.
But when you're in seventh grade,
that long term positive reinforcement of the good school
and the good job doesn't have that much control
over your behavior.
They need more immediate reinforcement.
And then there's reinforcement that's certain
and reinforcement that's uncertain.
Meaning reinforcement that's certain,
means every time I do it,
something positive happens.
In the real world,
that can happen in a laboratory with mice and pigeons.
But in the real world,
there's almost never certain reinforcement
meaning every time I know it's gonna happen

It's on some sort of a variable schedule.
But let's, for this lecture,

They didn't make hundreds of them

If you are a software engineer,

in personal communication skills, listening, and so forth.

to your team members, and to your partners
in the world of your work.

as a manager is to think of yourself as a coach,
because in fact, you do coach, just like the coach
of an athletic team, you are coaching the members
of your team to do the best they can do.
What is a coach?
A coach is somebody who is dedicated
to and concerned about the other person, the coachee,
if you will, the team member, the employee,
helping them to achieve their potential.
Your relationship with your employees
is not just about you getting them
to help you be successful,

your employees, be the absolute best they can be,
and they all have different potential,
they all have different talents.
But if they understand that you are dedicated
to helping them achieve their greatest potential,
they will help you achieve yours.
It comes, it's like throwing something against the wall.
It bounces right back.

as a coach and you have a client,

--- Asking Open-Ended Questions ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again, friends.
There's no more important coaching
and communication skill than your listening skills.
And there's passive listening and active listening.
In passive listening, you're basically,
you're putting the burden on the client
and you're basically saying, well,
they'll tell me whatever they wanna tell me.
Boom. You know, it's like their problem.
And I'll just sit here, you know,
you have nothing to say, okay, boom.
Well, obviously that's not healthy.
What's healthy is active listening.
And you maybe have already been trained in this
or be familiar with this, and that's fine.
But the most important part
of active listening is asking questions, right?
And the questions we ask people lead them in a direction.
No matter what it, if I ask you,
"How was your weekend?"
that leads you in a direction, right?
Whether you think it's good or bad,
maybe good at a point, right?
But you're basically saying,
"Let's talk about out of work things," right?
Let's talk about family, let's talk about sports.
You know, whatever.
So that question of, you know,
"How is your weekend" leads someplace, right?
As does every other question.
Now, in asking questions,
there are questions that are less significant,
and then they're what I'll call powerful questions.
And a powerful question is a question
that leads us towards an action,
that moves us towards the target, that creates commitment
to action towards target performance
or behavior that leads us in a direction
of achieving the challenge that the organization is facing.
So think about both asking open-ended questions
and asking powerful questions,
powerful questions that elicit the behavior that will get us
to performance, right?
I mean, we can think of a million questions to ask,
but they don't get us to improving the performance
of the individual or the team,
the behavior or the results, right?
And that's what we want to do.
So we should be thinking, you know,
even before we meet with somebody about, you know,
what are the powerful questions
that I should be asking during this session?
Now, so, you know, what's an open-ended question?
You probably already know this, but, you know,
a closed-ended question is one
that you can answer yes or no.
Did you go swimming this weekend? Nope.
Did you go to a movie this weekend? Nope.
Did you work this weekend? Nope.
Yeah, you see the point, all of those questions are closed
in that all the listener has to do is say no
and that's the end of the conversation.
Well, this is where you can play 20 questions, right?
I mean, to get anything meaningful, you know,
you can go through question after question.
You take a lot of time asking closed-ended questions.
Why not just say, "How was your weekend?"
"What did you do this weekend," right?
That's not a powerful question leading where we want to go,
but you, still, just the straight, the idea
of asking an open-ended question, and open-ended questions
lets the other person go where they want to go.
If you were asking somebody about their interests,
you wanna know what they're interested in.
Well, you know, you could say, you know, "Do you kayak?"
"Are you into photography?"
"Do you like dancing?"
You know, on and on and on, right?
Rather, you could ask 'what.'
You could simply say,
"I'd love to hear about your interests."
"What are the things you most enjoy doing?"
You can't say no to that, can you?
You've gotta offer something.
The listener, the other person can then go
where you might never think to go, right?
You know, they might say, you know,
"I enjoy going to auto races," you know.
"That's my favorite thing."
Well, you know, you might go through a hundred questions
before getting that.
So open-ended questions are simply efficient,
and they reveal what's on the mind
of the other person rather than what's on your mind.
A closed-ended question really, you know,
goes where your mind is going,
not where their mind is going.
Now, there is a time to ask a closed-ended question,
and that is where you know where you want them to go.
You know, like, you know, you could say,
"Has performance improved this week?"
Well, yes, that is a closed-ended question.
They could say no or yes, but it is powerful nonetheless.
And that is going where you want to go in terms
of focusing on performance.
But you might be better to ask 'what' instead
of just "Has performance improved?"
You might say,
"Tell me about performance this week.
What's happening?"
That's an open-ended question.
Sometimes you can say,
"Tell me," you know, or, "Can you talk about it?"
or something.
And it doesn't sound like a question initially,
but it really is a question.
You're asking them to elaborate on something.
So we have powerful and not powerful questions.
We have open-ended and closed-ended questions,
and we wanna be thinking about the open-ended questions
that are the more powerful questions.
You know, a powerful question is,
"When performance improved,
what were team members doing differently?"
You know, so that's an open-ended question.
You know, "What were they doing differently?"
If you said, "Were they doing anything differently?"
that would be a closed-ended question.
They could say yes or no, right?
But by saying, "When performance went up,
what were they doing differently?"
they're gonna have to think about that.
They're gonna have to give you something substantive.
And that question may lead them to take action.
It may lead them to say, well, you know what?
I don't know what they did differently.
I should ask them.
Good point.
That's what a powerful question can do.
It can lead you to inquiring, going to the Gemba, right?
Going to where the work is done
and inquiring about the right things
about what is really, really paying off in the organization.
"What could you do
to elicit that same behavior today or tomorrow?"
So again, that's a open-ended question,
but it's powerful in that you're asking the client
about what could they do to get that performance.
What could they do to get that behavior,
which then leads them to think about,
yeah, you know, I should do that.
Maybe I'll do that tomorrow, you know, or whatever.
But it's leading towards action, right?
Another one is, you know, what is the greatest obstacle
to team member motivation that you can impact?
So what is the greatest obstacle to their motivation,
you know, that you can impact, you know,
now, if you don't put that you can impact,
that could easily go off onto other things
about motivation that are out of their control.
But once you say what they can impact,
that's a powerful question because you're leading them
towards action that they could take, right?
Rather than other things they could, you know,
people love to talk
about things that are out of their control,
rather things that are in their control, right?
You know, that's what I, from my prison work,
my inmates loved to talk about the other guys, you know,
none of them belong there
because the other guys got them in trouble.
Well, we all have that, you know,
tendency towards externality, placing responsibility
for events in our life outside of us, not to somebody else.
Well, that's not productive.
That doesn't lead us to action.
So we wanna bring them internally, we wanna bring them
to what can they do to impact motivation.
And that can be a very fruitful discussion.
When do you use open-ended question?
Always good to start a conversation, right?
It gets a conversation going
because the other person needs to talk.
When you wanna learn about the interests
or the feelings of someone, when, you know,
the feelings of your client are very important to you.
'Cause people act on their feelings more than they act
on their thoughts, right?
We very often don't act on our knowledge or thoughts,
but we almost always
or very often act on how we feel about things.
So it's very worthwhile for you
to ask how they're feeling about performance,
how they're feeling about this part
of their team's behavior or whatever.
So asking about feelings, you know,
how are you feeling about, right,
is a way to phrase an open-ended question.
And here's some, you know, typical ways
that open-ended questions start.
"How can I help you?"
Or, "How can we help you?"
"What seems to be the problem?"
That's a open-ended question.
"What happened last night?"
Or, "What happened yesterday?"
"What happened last week?"
"You seem to be upset," you know.
"Why are you feeling the way you're feeling?"
"Why are you feeling upset?"
"Where were you when you first felt this way?"
Or, when did you first, you know, feel this way?
Those are all open-ended ended questions.
So think about for your next coaching session,
or your first coaching session,
think about framing open-ended questions,
and think about powerful open-ended questions
that will lead us in the direction
of our targets and challenges.
Make sense?

--- Reflecting or Rephrasing ---

(light music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let's talk about reflective listening or rephrasing.
Same thing, just different terms that are used
to describe this process.
Now, when we listen to someone,
we may have asked them an open-ended question.
And they have answered in response to that.
We have heard with our ears.
We have heard the sounds
coming out of the other person.
But have we understood what they really mean?
Have we understood what their ideas,
what their real feeling is,
what their judgment about that is?
Have we really understood?
And a way to clarify that
and to be sure that we are listening
to the meaning, not just the words,
but listening to the meaning
of what the other person has said,
it is useful to reflect that back.
And reflective listening,
if you just think about it physically,
you know, it's holding up a mirror to the other person,
saying, "Here's what I heard you say."
You know, "I heard you say that you find it difficult
to come to work
because you're so bothered by so and so.
Is that what I'm hearing?"
And they go, "Well, no, that's actually,
that's not what I meant.
I meant," good, good.
You know, when you rephrase or reflect back the meaning
of what you think the other person said,
to be wrong about that is a win, right?
I mean, winning in that reflecting game
is not about being right.
It's about learning more.
It's about understanding, gaining understanding.
So if you reflect back and the other person says,
"Well, that's not really what I meant.
What I meant was," great.
'Cause two things have happened when that happens.
First of all, the other person knows
you're really listening to them.
You're really trying to understand,
even if you've misunderstood.
You're really trying (laughing) to understand.
And you're giving them the opportunity to clarify
what you said.
And often, when you reflect back,
you know, people very often struggle
to say what they mean.
They struggle to talk about their feelings.
And when you reflect back,
you're actually giving them time and space

to think about what they said,

after you've reflected it back.
So there's the psychology of them feeling

You're attending to them

But there's also the substance, if you will,
of clarifying what they actually said.

what the other person has said or reflective listening

99% of the time, it's a win, (laughing) not a loss.
They'll appreciate it.
Unless your spouse is very different than mine.

"In other words," here comes the reflection.
Here comes the mirror.

to discuss this with your team.
Is that right?"
And then they'll say, "Yeah, that's right."

By the way, why is always a good open-ended question.
(laughing) Can't say no to why.

So okay, those are just some, you know,

When do you use rephrasing?
To clarify what the client has said

That's a clarification.
To help the client express their emotions.

--- Expressing Empathy ---

(gentle music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let's talk about empathy statements.
Now, when we did reflective listening,
we were for the most part reflecting
what we thought the other person said,
the meaning of what they said.
That's sort of a mind thing, right?
Sort of an intellect thing.
When we use empathy statements,
we're sort of reflecting back
what we think the other person is feeling
and why they're feeling it.
And so it's more of a heart.
You know, reflective listening is more of the head.
Empathy statements are more of the heart.
But in both cases, they're saying to your client
that you're hearing them,
that you're understanding both what they're thinking,
what they mean by their statements, and how they're feeling.
And why does it matter?
Why do we need to empathize?
Why do we need to express empathy
for the feelings of our client?
And the issue is very simply, human beings are emotional.
You know, I talked earlier in the course
about addressing the whole person.
Well, and we all differ in this.
Some of us are more head people
and some of us are more heart people, but we all have both.
And sometimes our heart or our emotions overcome our head
and our behavior is very often driven by how we're feeling
rather than some intellectual idea or how we're thinking.
So, and by the way, every marketing person,
every sales person understands that in buying a car,
for example, 75% of the decision or 80% of the decision
is an emotional thing.
It's about how I feel when I drive it,
how I'll feel in this new car,
rather than some intellectual idea about the horsepower,
emissions or something like that.
So, and that kind of governs how we behave.
So, an empathy statement expresses
how you think the other person is feeling
and why you think they are feeling that way.
And that, again, just like the reflective listening,
connects you.
It says, "I'm really listening to you."
We use empathy statements to reduce unhelpful emotions.
And let's just imagine you're coaching a team leader
and the team leader has a difficult team.
Now, whether it's a difficult team
or a difficult team leader, we don't know,
but from the the team leader's standpoint
whom we're coaching, they think their team is uncooperative
and they're not doing things they agreed to
and they're frustrated.
So the client, the team leader,
you know, goes, "Ah, they're driving me crazy.
"They're not doing this, and I'm gonna give up on them."
Maybe they express some anger, frustration, something.
So the coach says, "You sound very frustrated.
"Your team isn't following through
"on things they say they are gonna do,
"and that would be very frustrating, right?"
And they go, "Yeah, that is, right."
Now, it's not a big deal, is it?
But it's just connecting, saying,
"Yeah, I get your feeling.
"You know, you are frustrated. That feeling's legitimate."
That feeling may be about a person.
"You know, it sounds like that person really angers you
"when she does such and such," right?
So empathy statements all have,
the emotion you're hearing, like that reflective listening,
you're reflecting their emotion,
what emotion you think they're feeling,
and they'll acknowledge that.
They'll say, "That's right," you know, and why.
And the why is just as important as the emotion,
because the why then begins to lead to,
"So, what do we do about that?"
You know, "How do we resolve that?
"How do we solve that problem?"
Now, just a caution in using empathy statements,
and here's where performance coaching,
coaching leaders to succeed, what this course is about,
is a little bit different than life coaching.
We know where we want to go when we're coaching managers
in an organization, and where we want to go
is to improve performance and improve their skills.
Remember my mountain climber going up the steps.
And we don't wanna wallow in their emotions, you know?
There's a time we need to recognize their emotions,
but we wanna get past that as quickly as we can
without the person feeling
that we're dismissing their emotions.
Their emotions are legitimate.
We need to take them into account,
but we don't wanna stay there, okay?
We need to move on to, "So what's the action?
"What do we do about that?"
And that's not a bad thing to say, by the way.
So, "I hear you really frustrated because your team
"doesn't follow up on actions they agree to,
"so what could we do about that?
"What are some of the actions we could take
"to assure that they do follow up,
"to increase the probability at least
"that they follow up on action steps?"
And then you can brainstorm that together
or agree on some actions that the team leader can take.
But the important thing
is you want to get the team leader to act, right?
You want to get your client in motion,
moving towards developing a new skill
or improving the performance of his or her team.
So, empathy statements, you know, if you look at these,
these are typical structures of an empathy statement.
"It sounds as if you feel.
"It sounds like you feel.
"However...
"Because the team doesn't follow up
"on their action items, right?
"It sounds like you really feel frustrated
"because they're not following up on their action items.
"It must be frustrating.
"It must be annoying.
"It must be disappointing when
"blank, blank, blank," right?
"I can understand that you're frustrated.
"I can understand that," whatever.
Or, "I can understand that your team's not behaving
"the way you want them to behave.
"That would make you feel disappointed
"or frustrated or angry or whatever."
So those are all empathy statements,
and they're good
for helping the client get over an emotion,
to get past that emotion and get to action.
The danger, if you do not empathize
when your client is emotional,
they may be stuck there.
You know, they may not want to move on.
They may keep hammering at that frustration
because you haven't acknowledged it,
you haven't empathized with them.
So they want empathy.
They'll keep coming at you with their frustration
until you empathize with them.
But when you empathize, it sort of allows them,
it's like, "Okay, you got it.
"Now we can move on."
I hope that makes sense.
Empathy statements.
Another tool in your arsenal.
Thanks.

Acknowledging is going,

In a group setting, as a facilitator,
you know, just understand what we do in our culture.
At least, this is North American culture.
We don't like silence.
We have a great need to talk.
We're a noisy culture, right (laughs)?
There's noise all the time.
There's music on our iPad,
and there's music over the sound system, and there's TVs on.
And I mean, there's noise, noise, noise, noise, right?
And that noise sort of hinders our thinking in any deep way,
in any meaningful way.
And if you've ever noticed in a group setting,
if you're the facilitator, you try not to.
Let there be silence, just wait.
And you'll notice that you're tempted to jump in, right?
You wanna jump in.
Now, one danger for a coach is that you're smart.

You think you have a lot of ideas (laughs).
And you may, it may all be true,

You want the client to own things.
You want the client to own the idea,
the action plan, the credit for, right?

You've made an empathy statement, you know, let's say,

Half a minute, it's not that long.
Let them think and let something come from them.

So what I'm encouraging you to do is listen to yourself
and listen to see if you're just a little too anxious
to fill in the void and make noise, okay (laughs).

(gentle music)

--- Brainstorming Together ---

(light music)
-: Brainstorming.
Now, most of you watching this have probably
had some training in brainstorming.
And if you haven't, just very, very briefly,
the key point about brainstorming,
which we usually think of in a group setting, right,
with a team.
And you can brainstorm, you know, causes of a problem
or solutions to a problem, why it happened
and what we can do about it, right?
That's what we usually brainstorm.
And the key magic, if you will, or the chemistry
of brainstorming is putting aside judgments, right,
and just coming up with ideas.
And I know you may think, well why is there magic in that?
Well there is, there's some chemistry in that.
And in brainstorming you are free, even encouraged
to say wild and crazy things.
Why?
Not because that wild and crazy thing is what we might do,
but rather that wild and crazy idea
may stimulate an idea in somebody else
that might actually be the best idea, right?
So it's sort of a bouncing around chemical reaction
sort of thing, right?
So we're used to thinking about that in a group setting,
but could we one-on-one with a client, as a coach,
could we use brainstorming?
And the answer is yes, we could use brainstorming.
And it's a form of problem solving, right?
And you may be stuck on something,
what do we do about my team that won't do this, right?
Or what do we do about this other manager
who's not helping us out here?
Whatever the issue is.
And what the coach can do is say,
would you like to brainstorm this.
And now, if you're gonna brainstorm

So you have to say to the client,
why don't we brainstorm this,
or would you like to brainstorm this?
And then assuming that you know
that your client understands what brainstorming is,
if not you can give them a two minute explanation

and say, okay, how do we solve the problem
that your team doesn't show up on time,
or some members of the team don't show up?
Let's just say that's the problem.
How do we solve that?
What's, what's one idea?
And the client thinks for a second
and says offer them $10 every time they come on time.
Okay, you might write that down.
Don't judge it.
No judging, this is where the magic comes, right?
If you say we don't have money for that,
the moment you do that you're not brainstorming anymore.
So then you, then you, when you're the coach and client,
you the coach participate in the brainstorming, right?
I mean, you're sort of on an equal plane here
when you do this.
So you say, we could make a chart
and have stars every time somebody comes on time.
Okay, we could simply take roll call at the beginning
of the meeting and keep track of how many are on time.
The other person says, lock the door
two minutes after the time we're supposed to meet.
Anyway, you get the idea.
You go back and forth and then, you know,
when you've, you know, you do it for a little while, right,
you do it for a few minutes and then you say to the client,
do any of those ideas sound like they'll work for you?
And first ask them if they think
that's enough brainstorming, they'll probably say yes.
And then ask them what they think might work for them.
And then you go into action planning, right?
So you wanna try that?
They say, yeah, I'll try that.
So when do you think you'll try it?
You know what, when, right?
You always go through that.
Who's gonna do what, when?
That's action planning.
So you, the client, are gonna do that
at next Wednesday's meeting.
Yeah, I'll try that next Wednesday meeting, great.
You know, let me know how it goes next week.
So, you know, you've made the loop.
You've created the accountability.
Created an action plan.
You created accountability, right,
from the brainstorming.

Easy, but it's very useful sometimes.

--- Giving Feedback ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let's talk about giving and receiving feedback.
Now, why do we not give feedback?
You know, and let's be honest,
we're not all that courageous
and many of us don't give feedback,
'cause we've had bad experiences,
both giving it and receiving it, you know?
Many people have learned to hate the phrase,
can I give you some feedback,
'cause what does that mean?
It means I've criticism, is what it means, right?
Doesn't mean I'm gonna say something good.
It means I'm gonna criticize you in some way.
So, we do sort of have a immediate negative response
or fear, if you will,
when somebody's gonna give us feedback,
because of the way it's been given in the past.
So you need to establish a new pattern
with your clients where it's okay, where they expect,
they want your feedback, and they will want your feedback
if they feel that you are genuinely trying to help them.
That's the key thing.
You know, you're not there to criticize them.
You're there to help them.
So you're gonna say things like,
you know something that might work,
something that might be helpful to you,
something that that you might want to try, you know,
those are sort of positive suggestions
rather than, you know, you don't do this,
you don't do that, you know,
so the negative thing,
so you wanna try to structure your feedback
with positive actions that they could take.
So I want you to think
through your feedback before you give it, right?
And be sure that you are giving helpful feedback
and you're giving it in the spirit of caring
about the other person and helping the other person.
And they will sense that and telling them
what they could do rather than what they're doing bad
will definitely cause them
to digest feedback better.
But, you know, you may not give feedback,
because you may think, well,
everybody else doesn't seem to have a problem with that.
You know, I seem to be the only one who sees that.
Well, that's not good.
You may also sort of feel like, gee,
if I give 'em the feedback that might start an argument,
you know, I might start a negative interaction.
I might hurt their feelings.
Well, sort of overcome that
and overcome that by understanding what your job is
and your job is to be helpful, right,
to the other person and to help them improve.
And that will lead them in the right direction.
Now here's some guidelines for giving feedback.
Let's just go through these.
And as you're planning to give feedback,
you might remember these and sort of check them off
as you're planning to give somebody feedback.
Be sure of your intention, right?
Be sure that your intention is to be helpful
and be sure that what you're gonna say is gonna be helpful,
not simply critical.
Think it through.
Be clear, pinpoint, right?
Don't tell me that I have a bad attitude.
Don't tell me that I don't seem to care.
Tell me exactly what the behavior is
that you want me to change.
That's helpful.
It's not helpful to give me, you know, to tell me I'm lazy,
or to tell me something else about my personality.
I don't want to hear that.
Tell me what I can actually do
that will be helpful.
Emphasize the positive alternative.
We've talked about that.
Be very specific.
Avoid, you know, general comments like, you know,
spouses do this, okay?
You never, or you always, you never wash the dishes,
you always leave your socks on the floor.
No, I don't always leave my socks on the floor.
Last Thursday, I picked them up, right?
I don't know why it is,
but maybe only my spouse does, but spouses
tend to and saying, you never, or you always,
that's the way to start a fight, okay?
If you wanna start an argument and create bad feelings,

--- Receiving Feedback ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again.
Let's talk about receiving feedback,
and you'll probably be good at giving feedback
if you're good at receiving it, and vice versa, right?
If you're not good at receiving feedback,
you're probably not gonna be that skilled
at giving it either.
So I'd like you to think about
both sides of that interaction.
What makes somebody effective in receiving feedback?
Well, first thing, you know, accept the fact
that this person's trying to be helpful
and even if they're doing it badly, okay, (chuckles)
let's just assume they're terrible at giving feedback.
Every interaction we have with somebody
about our performance is a learning opportunity.
It's like every failure is a learning...
Entrepreneurs know this.
You know, every failed business idea is not a failure
unless you fail to learn from it, right?
Well, every attempt at somebody to give you feedback
is an opportunity, right?
Say, "Great, this is an opportunity for me to learn."
(chuckles) Now, it may not feel that way, right?
But work your head, you know,
try to get your head around the idea
that you want to learn, you want to improve.
This is what life is about, right?
So think about whatever this person says,
however clumsy they are at giving feedback.
Think about the opportunity in this feedback
as you're listening to the feedback.
And you can help the person giving feedback
by helping identify the specific behavior
that they're talking about.
And then, what can I do differently?
You know, how should I behave differently next time?
So if they don't give you good feedback
as to specifically what you could do next time

How could I fix that?
What do you think I can do differently?
Could I do this?
You know, what if I tried this?"
You know, so sort of, you know, start that...
You know, we wanna get to action.
We wanna get to better behavior.
We wanna improve our skills.
So, you know, use your coach.
You know, use the person giving you feedback
to help you learn and improve.
Ask for clarification.
You know, when the person says, you know,
"I think you should do this, and not this,"
or, "This doesn't seem to be working in your meetings."
Clarify it, you know, by saying,
"So what I'm hearing you say is that people
seem to be confused when I," blank, blank blank,
"do this, right?"
And they'll say, "Yes, that's what..."
or maybe no, then that's fine,
then let them clarify, right?
So problem solve together.
When somebody gives you feedback,
it shouldn't just be feedback.
It should be problem solving, right?
I mean, the feedback, the substance of what your coach
or your manager thinks you can do differently,
that's the beginning of a process.
And that process should very quickly go
into that problem solving process.
When does it occur? Why does it occur?
What can I do differently about it?
Summarize what you've heard from the coach
or the manager who's giving you feedback, or the peer,
or it could be a subordinate could give you feedback, right?
Summarize what you've heard and that is satisfying
and comforting to the person giving you feedback.
Because they wanna know that...
You know, the worst thing,
if you're giving somebody feedback
is they're going, "Really?
(chuckles) Okay, I got it."
You don't believe they've got it, right?
You don't believe they've really heard, right?
So feedback to them, summarize, you know,
what you've heard that you need to improve,
when does it occur, and what I'm gonna try,
what I'm gonna do differently.
And the person giving you feedback will go, "Great."
You know, that will make them happy, (chuckles)
that they have successfully given you feedback,
and they have successfully helped you.
Take responsibility.
We all have a tendency to blame the other guy.
Remember my prison story,
my inmates always blaming the other guy.
And the problem with my inmates was they didn't learn
and they didn't learn
'cause they were externals, not internals.
They were placing responsibility outside themself
rather than inside themself.
People who improve take responsibility,
and they're looking for what they can do differently,
not what somebody else did.
Somebody else may have screwed up too.
Somebody else may be a problem too,
but that's not my problem.
My problem is what I can do differently, right?
So let your coach or your manager know
that you are taking responsibility for your own behavior.
Let your manager or your coach know
that you are taking responsibility for your behavior,
and that will make you look good in their eyes.
I mean, to be perfectly honest,
we feel good about somebody who takes responsibility
for their performance, and it makes us very uncomfortable
when somebody is pointing the finger elsewhere
and not taking responsibility, so take that responsibility.
You know, when somebody gives you feedback,
it's not your annual performance appraisal,
a whole nother subject which I hate, but put that aside.
But it's not a total evaluation of you.
It's about a particular thing you can do differently.
You know, just keep in mind the world's best tennis player,
basketball player or musician, they have a coach.
And even though, you know, Steph Curry,
for the Golden State Warriors may be
the best basketball player today,
one of them, one of the two anyway.
During every game, his coach gives him feedback.
Now, it's not 'cause he is not good. He's great, okay?
But he's still getting feedback.
So getting feedback that you could do this differently
or you could improve on this, or you might try this,
it's not an evaluation of you.
It's just a suggestion about a specific behavior.
And if you keep that in mind,
it will make it easier to receive that feedback.
If you're thinking like, "Oh my God,
this person thinks I'm a terrible," whatever,
then you don't process the feedback well.
But if you take it as just a, you know, small component
of your overall performance, then it's easier to digest,
and it's easier for you to improve based on that.
Does that make sense?
(Lawrence claps)

It's a really important skill as both a coach

--- Solving Behavior Problems ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again, friends.
This lecture is as much a reminder as anything else.
You will remember when we talked
about developing skill and motivation,
we talked about the can do versus can or can't do
versus will or won't do.

is it a competency problem or a motivation problem?
That basic behavior analysis.

about discipline and solving problem behavior,
I wanna remind you about what I'll just refer

And this basically goes
through the thought process or the analysis.

in any what we call disciplinary process,
any kind of punishment,
as part of your counseling step,
when you talk to the person and you inquire

with the training, with the skill,
or perhaps they should be in a different job.

There's always a cost/benefit analysis
when you decide to develop a skill.

to play the violin,

They herd together.
And human beings are like that.

that the foundation of a lean organization is the team.
They said in the end
it's the dynamic work team that emerges
as the heart of the lean factory.
And that's because it's that team
that engages in continuous improvement.
It's that team that owns their process
and measures their process and worries about their process.

the entire organization will perform at a high level

and I don't know what your experience is

but there are really two kinds of teams,
and one kind of team is what were quality circles
or project teams.
Teams form to solve a problem, right?
They're not permanent, they're temporary teams
and they're not deciding
because when you form a quality circle
or a problem solving team,
they don't ultimately make the decision,
they go through the analysis of the problem

And the managing team then decides, yes,

That is a very different thing than a natural work team.
You know, if you worked on the family farm or a craft shop,
you didn't have problem solving groups.
The group who worked there
was the problem solving group, right?
I mean, the natural work team solved problems every day
as tools worked or didn't work or whatever,
figuring out how to solve a problem for a customer.
It was the people who actually did the work
who solved the problem.
Now, natural work teams own a process
from point A to point B,
as work flows through the organization.
Now, natural work teams occur
at every level in the organization.
In other words, we have a first level work team
doing the most value adding work in the organization,
the work that produces the output that goes to a customer.
But then there are helping teams, managing teams,
first level team coordinators or team coaches

The plant manager and her team is a natural work team.
They naturally are organized to solve problems
at that level
as is the board of directors of the company
and the president and vice presidents of the company.
They're all natural work teams.
And they all should follow a similar pattern.
And I'll tell you, one of the things I found most critical
in changing the culture of an organization.
Cultures are vertically integrated.
We imitate the behavior that's modeled above us, right?
The model of our parents influences our behavior.
Well, if you want to change the culture of an organization,
you can't just have great teams at the first level.
You have to have great teams at the next level
and the next level up the organization.
And they should all be using
good problem-solving techniques.
They should all have a scorecard.
They should all know what their processes are.
There should be a lot of similarity
in terms of how they function at each level,
that will achieve sustainability, right?
Very often, programs, right,
that create teams at the bottom
don't sustain and they don't sustain
'cause the people above
are not practicing the same practices.
But if they adopt similar, if not identical practices,
they get it and they'll understand it
and they'll support it
and they'll sustain it at the next level.
As a new manager, it's important for you to understand,
I don't know, of course what your work situation is,
but it's important for you to understand
that most work today gets done in teams.
Software development is not a solo job.
Software development gets done in teams,
creating almost anything, almost every creative act today,

But there are some jobs like writing either a book

But almost all work is done in teams.
So the degree to which you become a effective team leader,
you're more likely to move up the ladder of management

You'll be more successful.
Now, just a very brief history about teams,
back in the 1950s and early 1960s,
the idea of self-directed or self-managing teams
started to develop.
And it developed at the Tavistock Institute first,
a guy named Fred Emery and Eric Trist
were the sort of the first important thinkers about this.
And what they did was there was a coal mine in Scotland
and they were implementing new technology in the coal mine.
And the owner of the mine asked these professors
at the Tavistock Institute
if they would help develop an organizational structure
around the new technology.
This is important, social system,
technical system go together.
Very important idea.
And they looked at the technical system
and they said we don't really know the best way
to organize around that.
Who should we ask about that?
Who might we involve in thinking about that?
Now, just long history in coal mines.
Coal mines are dangerous places.
In the British Islands,
when they worked in coal mines 200 years ago,
they went down in family units.
You trust your brothers, you trust your family, right?
And when you work in a dangerous environment
you want people around you whom you trust.
Modern methods, so-called modern methods
meaning Henry Ford's factory modern methods
came to coal mining.
And with it,
the separation of decision making from doing, right,
now we had supervisors, they divided up,
specialized the tasks.
So we weren't working in a team,
we were managed individually.
And you know what else came?
Union strife, conflict, safety problems.
The whole culture of mining went downhill.
But these guys at the Tavistock Institute asked the miners,
they got the miners together and they said,
how would you like to organize to do this work?
Understanding what the new work will be
with this new technology,
how would you like to organize?
And guess how they organize themselves.
They organize themselves sort of back
into those family units or into teams.
And they implemented that
and they studied the learning from that.
And these are some of the lessons
that they derived from that.
They found that it's best to think about the work being done
by a whole group,
a team rather than managing individuals.
This is the herding cats
versus working with a high-performing team.
They found that the work group
could control their own work internally within the team
if they were empowered to do that.
They found that if team members were multi-skilled,
in other words, if you have, in a factory,
you have a work cell where there are eight different jobs,
the ideal case is that all the members
of the team can do all eight jobs.
Then when one is absent,
one gets sick for one reason
or if one falls behind the other,
the others can step in and do that person's job.
This creates continuous flow
because now you have flexible workers.
It's like on a baseball team,
every coach would like to have a utility infielders,
an infielder who can play third base, shortstop,
second base, first base, or even the outfield.
The more flexible, the more multi-skilled they are,
it gives you more options in terms of managing the team
and how the team functions.
It also enables people to think better
about how to solve problems if they are multi-skilled.
And the other thing that happens
is when team members rotate, do different jobs,
the job becomes more satisfying
rather than more boring and mundane.
If you're doing the same exact thing
day in, day out, repetitively,
your brain kind of dies on you, right?
But if you rotate, then it's much more interesting,
it's much more fun and your brain is alive
and you can solve problems better.
So those are some of the basic ideas
of the team structure.
And let's go on and talk
about how to really create that structure
and how to create a high-performing team.
(light guitar music)

--- Stages of Team Development ---

(cheerful music)
-: Hi again, folks.
Let's talk about stages of team development,
and there's a model that is very common.
If you have have any training and teamwork at all,
I'm sure you've heard this model.
It was really begun in the 1930s
by a professor who studied the development of teams,
and there are four stages of team development.
I'm gonna give you a model that I've developed
that I also, I like.
Maybe I just like it 'cause I developed it,
but I think other people have found it helpful as well.
Well, when you first form a team, there is a forming period,
and that's where people are figuring out their role
and the role of other people and relationships.
You know, who are you?
Who am I?
Who makes decisions?
You know, is somebody bossy?
Is somebody domineering?
Is somebody wimpy and never speaks up?
And are some people working harder than others?
And you know, just what's the deal here, right?
I've just moved into this house called this team,
and I'm sensing, I'm figuring out.
And you get everybody figuring things out
and people tend to bump into each other a little bit,
and it's okay.
You know, you just gotta go through that forming stage.
And then there's a storming stage,
and the storming stage
is where people may rebel against responsibility.
Say, "Well, it's not my job.
Why do I care about that, right?"
And they may bump into each other, right?
And they may have mini fights.
I won't call 'em real fights,
but they may say, "Hey, wait a minute!
That's my territory.
You stay in your own territory.
That's my job.
It's not your job."
Or, "How come you're not doing that?
You're supposed to be doing that."
Or, "You're giving me lousy stuff,"
or, "You're messing me up," or something.
You know, there's a whole sort of, there's a conflict stage.
And rather than freak out over that,
it's useful for you to know that it's normal.
Almost every team goes through that,
and almost every team gets out of that.
Now, you're not just passive
as they go through the storming.
You're helping to resolve conflicts.
You're helping to make sure people do know
what their roles and responsibilities are,
that some people may need feedback
in terms of their communication with other people, right?
That may not be that helpful.
So you're giving them that feedback.
Remember, our giving and receiving feedback skill
will really come in useful during this storming stage.
But you seek to clarify things,
and you are now helping people
learn how to work together well.
And then there's sort of a calming down, right?
A norming stage,
where people sort of settle into their roles
and they settle into their relationships,
and they begin to function,
and then there's the performing stage,
where people really are now,
everything becomes fluid, if you will,
and people begin to perform at a higher level.
They've resolved their conflicts.
They understand each other, they know each other's jobs,
and they will go on to focus on the performance,
focus on the data,
and that's the really productive stage.
So you want to get them through those first three stages
as quickly as possible,
and your job is to be the coach, right?
You are a coach,
and you're gonna coach them through those stages
to get to that performing stage,
and that's when you're really focusing on the process
and you're focusing on improving the process
and solving problems.
This is a model I came up with a few years ago
that describes maturation,
a team becoming mature and who makes what decisions.
You know, initially when you're raising children,
and your child is two years old or three years old,
do you say, "Would you like to decide on the menu tonight?"
What you might find
when you ask your two-year-old to decide on the menu,
you're gonna have ice cream first,
and then you're gonna have cake,
and then you're gonna have more ice cream.
That's gonna be the menu, right?
Well, no, no, no, no, no.
That's not gonna be the menu.
Mommy and daddy decide on the menu,
and the menu is we're gonna have a salad,
and you're going to eat some salad,
and then we're gonna have the main course,
and then after you eat those,
(chuckles) every parent's been through this, right?
After you eat those, then you may have dessert,
which might be ice cream, okay?
That's gonna be the menu.
Now, so mom and dad are pretty controlling, aren't they?
When the child is immature.
But what do you do as your child develops good judgment,
develops responsibility and maturity?
What you do is you,
hopefully, you turn decision making over to them.
You back off, if you will,
and you allow your 14-year-old daughter
to decide when she's gonna do her homework,
because she has taught you,
you have learned that she cares about her homework,
and she will get her homework done
because she feels that responsibility.
That's what you want, right?
So when she is there at that point of taking responsibility,
you as a mom or dad, you don't be pushy.
You back off.
You have now sort of delegated responsibility.
She's becoming more autonomous.
The object of a parent is to develop children
who are completely autonomous, right?
They're gonna leave the house,
they're gonna be on their own,
and you're not gonna be telling them what to do,
so you want to develop that maturity of decision making.
Now, you back off and give them responsibility
as they demonstrate.
There's sort of a left foot, right foot.
First you teaching them what responsible behavior is,
them demonstrating that behavior,
and then you backing off, right?
But do you say at the point of,
say they're 16, and they legally can drive,
"Well, they're 16, they can drive.
They can do what they want."
And they come in at three o'clock in the morning
with your car?
No, no, no, no.
Not my children, anyway.
No, no, no.
We agree on what time you will come in.
You take the car, and you need to be back
at the time you have said that you're gonna come back,
let's call it 11 o'clock at night,
and you have to demonstrate that.
And if you don't demonstrate that responsibility,
if you come in at three in the morning,
you lose the car keys, okay?
That's it for two months or some period of time.
You're in timeout.
You don't get the car.
Now, understanding that progression, right,
from the bottom left is low taking responsibility
and high control by the manager,
in that case, being the parent, right?
When a new employee comes in,
until they demonstrate their responsibility,
you're gonna be more controlling.
You're gonna be more observing on a high frequency, right?
But as they demonstrate their responsibility,
you'll more back off and let them take that responsibility.
The same with the team.
You know, when you create a team,
and even if you call it a self-directed team,
which is a misnomer,
'cause there's no such thing as a self-directed team.
As long as there's a chairman of the board
and a CEO of the company,
no team is self-directed.
Every team takes direction.
The plant manager,
the president of a company takes direction.
So nobody's self-directed, right?
But they become more autonomous
as they demonstrate that responsibility.
Now, the two defects that occur
is the manager backs off, right?
And says, "Well, you know,
you're supposed to be responsible.
Do what you want.
I can't tell you what to do."
That is permissive, right?
That's that lower right corner of that matrix,
and that produces performance problems.
On the other hand, if the manager is over-controlling,
wanting to make too many decisions
when the team has demonstrated their responsibility,
that demotivates the team
and the team then says, "Well, the heck with it, you know?"
So think about your relationship to your team
in terms of what autonomy you're giving them.
Are you developing that team
so they are moving northeast on that matrix?
Are you teaching them what responsible behavior is?
Are you creating the situation
where they take responsibility for their performance?
When we talk about the team scorecard, and so forth,
that will help create that.
But that is your job.
Your job is to move them northeast,
just as your job as a parent
is to develop highly responsible, autonomous children.
I hope that makes sense.
Thanks.

--- Roles and Responsibilities on A Team ---

(bright music)
-: Let's define the roles and responsibilities on your team.
Now, you may take this for granted.
You may say, "Well, wait a minute.
I'm the leader of the team, so I facilitate the team.
I conclude that the team has agreed on these things.
I hold people accountable.
I take notes on what we agreed to.
I have the watch.
I keep track of the time.
I do all those things 'cause I'm the leader of the team."
If you wanna make your life difficult,
you can have that attitude,
and if your team is very immature, that may be right.
But as your team matures, remember the teenager growing up,
delegate things to other team members.
And let me just give you an example.
I was working with the president and his team
of the exploration and production group,
about 6,000 people, of one of our largest oil companies,
and I coached that team for about a year.
And the president
of this exploration and production company,
when I observed first observed their meeting,
they had a long rectangular table,
and guess where the president sat?
Of course, he sat at the head,
quote, the head of the table, right?
The power position.
And it was his meeting, you know,
and everybody else was just sort of there to please him,
and you could, if you observed,
it wasn't really a team meeting.
It was more of a one-on-one reporting,
but in a forum for public humiliation
is what it really was, right?
It wasn't really a shared sense of,
you know, we all own this as a team.
So as we developed the team,
I suggested that he not facilitate every meeting.
I mean, it was clear he was the boss, okay?
Nobody disputed or was confused about that,
but does the formal leader have to lead every meeting?
Is the skill of facilitating a meeting
something that's only owned by the formal leader?
In fact, this guy was the president, all right?
His people on his team were vice presidents.
Well, they were also learning
team facilitation skills, right?
So it wasn't like he was the only one
who was gonna be good at facilitating the team.
So I suggested that at the end of the next meeting,
he asked who would like to facilitate
next week's or next month's.
It was actually a monthly meeting, but next month's meeting.
And that was sort of a surprise to the group,
because they always, they just assumed this is his meeting.
And he sort of had some trepidation about this.
He wasn't sure this was a great idea.
But I can tell you that after a few months,
he loved this because it relieved him of the burden
of always facilitating the group,
and he could observe
how other members of his team facilitated the group,
and he could sort of sit back and observe
and really act as a member of the team
and be thinking more
about the substance of the conversation,
rather than just his role as the leader of the team.
So that became the normative practice.
And I've done this with dozens and dozens of teams,
where the leader shares that facilitation responsibility,
so there are different roles on a team,
and one, of course, is always the formal leader of the team,
which let me assume that's you for the sake of argument.
But then you may have different people
acting as the facilitator of the team,
and we'll talk more in coming lectures
about what that facilitator does.
But you can also have somebody
who's the note taker, the scribe,
and just the note about being the note taker or the scribe.
You're not a court recorder.
In other words, you don't record everything people say.
In fact, that's a very bad thing to do,
because you want free-flowing conversation
and you want people to feel comfortable
saying things that 10 minutes later,
they may recognize themself as a rotten idea,
but they threw it out 'cause it was an idea.
But you don't want people held accountable.
Once you write something down,
you know, people are held accountable for that,
and that's a little intimidating,
so that will make people very hesitant to speak out.
So the scribe writes down,
has the agenda and their decisions and action items.
So the scribe writes down, what did we decide, right?
And what's the action plan?
We'll talk about action planning a little bit later,
but there's a what, a who, a when, you know, action plan,
and then you come back to that at the next meeting, right?
But the scribe does that.
And then I'm gonna give you an agenda, by the way.
I'm giving you a lot of good stuff.
I'm going to give you an Excel spreadsheet
that's an agenda,
and on the agenda, there are times allocated
for each item
that we're going to talk about this week, let's say,

and what the timekeeper does,
if there's 15 minutes allocated for a topic,
you know, with three minutes to go,
the timekeeper may have a flip chart
and just gets up and writes three, you know?
And everybody knows, "Oh, three minutes.
Okay, let's make a decision.
Let's get on with this."
And then at one minute, writes one,
and then goes like this for a timeout.
Our time's up on this.
It's good to separate that from the facilitator role,
because the facilitator's thinking about engaging people
and are people really having the opportunity
to share their thoughts,
and are people understanding what somebody is saying?
You know, there's a whole skill to that facilitation role
that you need to pay attention to,
you need to be focused on.
So you can easily lose track of the time
and lose track of the note keeping
if you're doing all those roles at once.
There may be other subject matter experts.
You know, you may decide that every week on your team,
you're gonna look at the quality data,
so maybe somebody's the owner of that quality data
and shares that at each meeting with the team.
That would be a subject matter expert.
And there might be
other subject matter experts on your team.
So what I'd like you to do
is I'd like you to consult with your team
about these different roles and responsibilities
and decide how you're gonna handle
those roles and responsibilities.
Are you going to do it all as the team leader?
It's up to you, but I don't recommend that.
Get the monkey off your back, right?
Share responsibility with your team,
and that makes 'em a better team.

--- The Agenda ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again, folks.
Let's talk about the agenda for team meetings.
Now, first of all, what kind of meetings are you having?
And my recommendation, if you're in a manufacturing plant,
and you're at the first level in the organization,
I suggest you have a daily 10 to 15 minute huddle
at the beginning of shift with your team.
And you're gonna be at the visual display.
Some people call it a huddle board.
And you're gonna go over the data,
you're gonna go over any changes
that were made from yesterday,
anything that we want to experiment with,
any ways we want to try to improve things today.
And that's a quick meeting every day.
And you have an agenda for that.
Now, another kind of meeting that you may have,
and if you're at the next level of management,
or even if your team is not, you're not in a factory,
you may have a weekly meeting.
I know a lot of teams have virtual meetings
'cause they've got people spread out.
So it's on WebEx or Skype or some other,
you know, online meeting tool.
You must have an agenda.
People need to come to the meeting
knowing what the expectations are,
knowing what topics are gonna be covered.
And that makes for an efficient meeting.
If you or other team members don't know
what we're doing at this meeting, we kind of wander around.
And as Yogi Berra said, "If you don't know
"Where you're going, you may end up somewhere else."
And that's kind of true.
So you want to know where you're going,
and you want to know what the order of things is,
and you want to know how much time
you have allocated to each topic on the agenda.
Now, attached to this lecture,
I am giving you a great gift.
And it's a great gift.
It's an Excel spreadsheet.
And at the bottom, you know, I suggest you open it up now,
pause the video and open it up.

the first tab is the agenda.

Now, let's look at the agenda just for a second.
Sort of highlighted in light blue color
are standard agenda items.
Now, you can decide that these are the right
or the wrong standard agenda items,
but I'm telling you what has worked for a lot of teams.
The first thing you do on the agenda is go over the agenda,
and you ask the team members whether they agree
to this agenda or whether they have other items.
Is there other things they'd like
to problem solve or discuss or go over.
In a manufacturing plant, very often,
safety first, there's a quick safety conversation.
Then there's recognition.
And I know this may seem like an odd thing to you,
but this is sort of part of the four to one philosophy.
You know, we're really good at criticizing people
and catching people doing the wrong things,
but we're not so good at catching
people doing the right things.
So it's worth taking a minute,
and it doesn't have to be more than a minute,
to ask yourself the question has anybody
done anything right.
Has anybody done anything worthy of some comment
or recognition in the past week
or whatever the period is since our last meeting?
And that's not, you know, it doesn't have to come from you.
You know, you ask the whole team,
you know, is there any recognition?
Does anybody have any recognition?
And one team member may recognize another team member
for something he or she did that was helpful to them,
or some new tool they came up with,
or form they came up with,
or procedure they came up with, or some problem they solve.
So that's a good way to start a meeting,
right after sort of what is the agenda, safety,
and then recognition.
Then action plan review.
Now, if you look at the tab on the bottom,
the action plan tab, just go to that for a second.

in your meetings, you're gonna fill this in.
I suggest you have a laptop, your scribe,
whoever's fulfilling that role, have his or her laptop.

to the next meeting, that's a decision.

there on that second tab, that should be the record

different action items that different people
are gonna take responsibility for.
And so you're gonna just ask people
who's gonna do that?
And John, when do you feel like you can have that done by?
And John said, well, I can have it done by next week.
Fine, you put the date of next week down there.
Now very, very, very, very important.
When you develop an action plan,
at your next meeting, I'm gonna be very
authoritarian on this, you must go over the action plan.
And here's why.
If you develop an action plan and people have agreed
to do things, and you don't come back and talk about it,
guess what's gonna happen, people aren't gonna do it.
People will forget about it,
and it'll become absolutely waste,
wasted time, wasted effort.
You won't make progress.
You must come back at each meeting,
review the action items that people have agreed to.
And this is not to beat people up if they haven't done it.
You know, it's simply to say John, you said,
your action item was to do this, how'd it go?
And John says, I forgot about that, I wasn't able.

you're gonna come back to it.

Discipline, remember, having a disciplined team.

--- Team Facilitation - Why It Matters! ---

(soft music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let's talk about facilitation and resolving conflicts.
This is a really, really, really important skill
for any manager, and particularly for any manager
who leads a team of people.
Your ability to facilitate conversation, to facilitate unity
of purpose and problem solving and moving forward
by your team will largely determine your success.
Think about all the skills that you've already learned
in terms of active listening, rephrasing,
asking open-ended questions, making empathy statements.
All of those skills, there's a reason they came
before this section,
because in this section we're gonna employ

and you go back and you do, because they are critical
to facilitation and they are critical to resolving problems.
You have to be able to employ those skills.
In this lecture, I'd just like to talk about why
facilitation matters and what it is.
To facilitate is to enable others to participate,
to enable others to make their contribution.
And if you have 10 people in the room
everybody has unique perspective,
a unique background, unique experience.
And what you're trying to do when you're
facilitating a group is you're trying
to create collective wisdom.
If you just think, there's some problem, right?
And if somehow you could get out of those 10 people
all of their knowledge and experience and ideas,
and put it in the middle of the room without any egos
attached, where it's not my idea,
I contribute my idea and I turn it over to the group.
And it becomes the groups then to shape, to mold, to change.
And out of that process of summing up the knowledge
and wisdom experience of all the members
of the group, you come out
with something that no one person could have come out with.
And that's the objective of facilitation.
That's the objective of team problem solving.
So to facilitate, facil,
the root word means to make easy
and you're trying to make it easy for others.
When you're a good facilitator
you are highly aware of how your behavior affects others.
You know how you look at people, whether you nod your head
when they're making a contribution
or whether you're going...
Your behavior facilitates
or does the opposite causes people not to participate.

--- Team Facilitation Skills 2 ---

-: Hi again, friends.

"You caught me.

Those kind of statements where you're expressing empathy
for the person, help people overcome emotion.
And you'll have a lot

Not much emotional heat, if you will,
but there will be times
and this sort of leads to
the conflict situation where people really
have an emotional investment in something.

And then,

I mean, this is difficult for him.

I mean, you really do know how to do your job

but the fact that you have understood.
Remember, everybody is right through their eyes,
from where they sit.
So from where he sit, he hasn't said something wrong.
He said something right.
Given his history, his life.
So empathizing with that,
appreciating that then makes it easier for him to move on
and go along with what may be a group decision.
Sometimes when there's emotional temperature in the room,
where people sort of get hot about things,
it's worth just sort of stopping.
And particularly if there're two people,
this sort of gets into the conflict resolution.
But if there are two people who have opposing views,
it's a good idea to sort of take the conversation away
from the two of them for a moment
and say to the rest of the group,
guys how are the rest of you feeling about this?
And then a third, fourth,
fifth person can make their contribution.
It can cool down and you're creating comfort in the group.
And that will help each
of the two individuals be more comfortable.
There's a time to play cop.
I don't know how else to put it,
but you are the leader of the group.
You are the the manager of this team, I'm assuming.
And sometimes the team needs to know where
their boundaries are.
And sometimes you will need to say,
"Hey guys, I think we're talking
about something that we can't do anything about.
This really is a matter
that has to be decided at a different level.
So let's just kind of move
on to a different topic.
Now you're not asking them
for their opinion about that, right?
You're not trying to engage them.
You're actually trying to disengage them
by saying that's sort of an out of bounds discussion.
You may not use the phrase out of bounds,
but you may say, I think, we need to move
on from that 'cause it's something we can't control.
Or it may be just a time element
and you say, "Hey, guys,

Again, that's sort of, you're controlling,
you're playing playing cop.
And generally,
there may be some time when this isn't true,
but I found 95% of the time people appreciate that,
that they appreciate you doing that,
when in fact it's right, when in fact we need to move on.
We are running out of time.
Or in fact, that's something that we cannot decide.
So a final thing that you do is concluding,
and this may be one of the most useful facilitation skills
and it sounds almost silly,
but we have to bring things to a conclusion.
You can call this summarizing also.
And in every meeting there's a time when
there is this natural tendency to just keep
talking about things.
And your job as the facilitator is to bring
the conversation, whether it's on a topic
or the whole meeting to a conclusion by saying,
guys, let me summarize what where I think we are.
Let me summarize the decisions I think we've made
or let me conclude this topic.
I think we've all gotten to the same point.
I think we have a consensus.
I think we're agreeing that we should boom and people will
oh yeah, I think we are in agreement with that.
So let's make that decision and let's move on.
Or you may say, let's make that decision and let's decide.
Now let's go to action planning.
Let's decide who's gonna do what to whom when.
That's that action planning step that's on your agenda.
Remember your agenda with the second page,
your action register.
That's a way of concluding the conversation.
These things are really important
because they make meetings efficient.
Meetings aren't efficient because people don't talk.
Meetings aren't efficient just 'cause of a timeline.
Meetings are efficient because you use mental energy well
and you don't waste time, you don't waste words,
you don't waste mental energy.
That's part of your job as as a facilitator.
So I hope those first seven facilitation skills
are are helpful to you.
Let's go now and talk about maybe the harder part
of conflict resolution, why we have conflicts
and what we can do about it.
Thanks.
