--- The Enron Story and the Abilene Paradox ---

(upbeat music)
-: I wanna share a quick story with you about Enron
and what Jerry Harvey called the Abilene paradox.
And I think it's a really important lesson
for a group facilitator or for a whole team
or anybody who's on a team.
The Enron Corporation, and I forget the years now,
but if you go back and study Enron,
one year, according to Fortune Magazine,
it was the world's most admired company.
The board of directors of Enron
was considered the most admired board of directors.
On the board were only two insiders,
total of 14 members, 12 of them outsiders.
And on the board, there were presidents
of mutual fund companies, presidents of insurance companies.
There were people, there was a professor
who was the dean of a business school
and had written accounting books.
He had been an accounting professor.
This board really knew finance.
They really knew accounting.
They were a really good board of directors
by any objective measure or understanding
of what a board of director should be.
A year later, Enron declared bankruptcy.
And you say, "How the devil does that happen?"
Well, Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, professor at Yale University,
studied the Enron case and particularly the board.
And here's what he found.
They would go to board meetings
and they would be given financial statements
and they would all look at them and the CEO
and the chief financial officer would explain
how great everything was,
how well things were going.
And they would read them and they would look
around the room and nobody else was questioning them.
So I might, you know,
let's say I was the accounting professor.

and what was off the books
and where was debt and some questions
that are really important.
But because nobody else was asking,
nobody else saw any problem.
I sort of said, "Well, guess it must be okay."
It wasn't okay.
They were not asking probing questions.
They were acquiescing and they were not inquiring.
And because they acquiesced and they didn't inquire,
they didn't drill down, they didn't probe,
even though they had questions in their mind,
they went along with what looked like
would be approved by the group.
There was group think going on at the Enron board
and it destroyed the corporation.
Jerry Harvey was a professor at Washington University

I recommend it highly.
He wrote another book that very few people have read,

"How Come Every Time I Get Stabbed in the Back,
My Fingerprints Are On The Knife."
Good title, right?
Good book, too.

The Abilene paradox goes something like this.
Jerry Harvey tells the story.
He grew up in Coleman, Texas,
down the road from Abilene, about 40 minutes down the road.
And this was in the 1950s.
And he was a little boy sitting on the front porch.

it's really hot in August, okay?

And the phrase on the road to Abilene
has become a important phrase.
They're on the road to Abilene
with the windows rolled down, dust is blowing.
And halfway to Abilene, ma says to pa,
"Why the hell are you dragging us to Abilene?"
And pa says, "I ain't dragging you to Abilene.
You wanted to go to Abilene."
She says, "No, I didn't.
I didn't wanna go to Abilene.
You wanted to go to Ab-"
And suddenly, nobody in the car
really wanted to go to Abilene,

And the question is,

Jerry Harvey, as he tells the story,
he's sitting in his office at the university,

and this young woman comes in, student,
and she looks depressed.
And he says, being the sensitive person that he is,

And he says, "Well, why are you marrying somebody

And I couldn't say no.
And he told his parents, and they called my parents

And if I backed out now, I would break all their hearts.
I just can't do it."
The next day he is in his office.
This young man comes in, he looks depressed
and he says, "Pray tell, why are you depressed?"
And he says, "Well, you'd be depressed too
if next Sunday you were marrying somebody you don't love."
And he says, "Oh, well,
why are you marrying somebody you don't love?"
And he says, "Well, it was a moment of passion.
And I couldn't say no.
And, you know, I told my parents
and they called her parent and I couldn't back out now.
It'd break our heart.
It would break their hearts."
They're in the bus.
They're all on the road to Abilene.
Both families are on the road to Abilene.
And I won't bore you with any more story,
but Jerry Harvey goes on to tell a whole bunch
of stories about corporate boards like Enron.
He doesn't tell that 'cause it happened afterwards.
But there are lots of stories
of people being on the road to Abilene.
Now the question is, is your team on the road to Abilene?
Are you on a team that's on the road to Abilene, right?
Well, you wanna make sure
that you're not like that Enron board,
that you're facilitating the group
and participating in the group in a way
where you are absolutely frank and honest
and you don't get on the road to Abilene.
You don't acquiesce, you inquire.
It's never wrong to ask questions.
It's never wrong to probe.
And I believe that as a member of a team,
you have a moral obligation.
Yes, use the word moral.
Your job on any team is to help
that team achieve its goals and perform well
in a way that satisfies the needs
of all the team members.
If you have knowledge that you're not sharing
or you have questions that you are not sharing
with the team, you are cheating your team.
Don't cheat your team.
Don't acquiesce.
Your job is to be absolutely frank and honest
with your team and share your knowledge,

--- How To Gain Consensus ---

(bright upbeat music)
-: Hi folks. I'd just like to take a minute
to talk about how to reach a consensus.
And I found that some teams
do this very easily, quickly, and naturally.
And there's some teams and team leaders who struggle
with how to reach consensus with their group.
And it doesn't have to be that hard.
Some people make it hard,
but it doesn't have to be that hard.
And let me suggest to you
that there there are two ways
of thinking about that word, a theory of consensus.
And I've run into consultants from academia,
who think about consensus in an academic way.
And when trying to apply that in a business setting,
that can be somewhat of a disaster.
And their thought about consensus is,
everybody must reach agreement.
Everybody has to get their,
and we have to talk about it.
Whatever the decision is,
we have to talk about it
until everybody has the same understanding,
and everybody agrees that decision A,
versus decision decision B, is the right decision.
And until then, we can't say we have consensus.
Now, in the academic world, that may make sense.
It's a good definition of consensus.
But I live in the business world.
And in the business world,
there's an economy involved.
We can't take forever to make decisions.
Even consensus decisions.
There has to be judgment involved.
And, we have to use what I will call practical consensus.
And I wanna define practical consensus,
and give you a way to get there.
Practical consensus is when first,
we've shared the decision, the problem,
we've shared all the facts,
everybody understands the facts,
everybody has an opportunity to inquire,
to ask questions, right?
Then we share alternatives.
Everybody gives their ideas
about different courses of action that we might take.
Everybody's had an opportunity to argue a case
for one or the other.
We've considered the pros and cons.
And everybody's voiced their opinion,
as to which way we should go.
Now, if you're lucky, everybody comes out,
saying we should go left and not right, great.
But what if we have a group of 10,
and seven say we should turn left,
and three more say no, we should turn right.
What do you do?
Do we have consensus?
Well, here's what we have to do then.
The first thing we have to do,
is we have to ask the three, why.
Tell us what you see as a problem
with turning left, versus turning right,
or whatever the decision is.
And maybe they have a reason
to have a different view that's valid,
and needs to be heard by the other people.
So they express that view.
And the other seven people say, "We understand."
Now they might change their mind, and that's fine.
But more likely they'll say,
"You know, we understand.
We did understand that,
but we still think turning left
is the right way, and here's why."
And the three who said,
"No, we think we should turn right."
They've expressed their view,
and they've heard the view of the seven.
And now, "Does anybody want to change their opinion?"
Now, here's the two critical elements
of reaching practical consensus are,
we have to be absolutely frank and honest
in sharing our ideas,
sharing what we think is right or wrong, or so forth.
But then the second,
and this is the critical ingredient
that is often lacking.
And this is the basis of democracy, by the way.
We have to be willing to sacrifice.
We have to be willing to say,
"That's not what I think is the best decision,
but I'm willing to go with the group."
Right. "And I'm willing to support
what the group decision is,
as if it were my own decision.
I'm gonna support it enthusiastically.
The moment I can do that,
then we'll have consensus."
We'll have what I call "practical consensus."
And yes, there's the element of sacrifice.
There's the element of absolute honesty and frankness,
there's the element of listening, respect,
and then there's their willingness to sacrifice.
And if you think about how democracy should work,
that's how they should work, right?
When we go through an election,
or we we're voting for a bill in Congress,
or the parliament, you know,
we voice our opinions, we're absolutely honest,
we make our case.
But then once we vote,
we have to be willing to,
to go along with the group,
and support it, and hope it works, right?
But you'll never know if it works,
if you don't support it.
If people undermine it, by their lack of support,
that's not good.
That's not good team play, right?
When a football team goes out,
and, you know, in the huddle,
somebody may say, "Hey, I'm open."
You know, "Pass the ball to me over here."
The quarterback may say, you know,
"Well thanks for telling me,
but instead, we're going to run this other play."
Now the the wide receiver who wanted to catch the ball,
he has to be willing to say,
"I'm a team player."
You know, "I go with how the play was called."
So I hope that that makes sense.
And here are just sort of some criteria,
or even steps in reaching consensus,
clarifying the issue,
making sure all facts are on the table,
making sure everybody's informed,
everybody's given the opportunity to speak.
And respectfully, they are heard.
All opinions are considered equitably,
meaning with equal voice.
We don't just view the opinions as being greater merit,
because somebody's more senior,
or somebody has higher rank.
The pros and cons are weighed,
and then there's that willingness to sacrifice.
Now, let me just share a very, very, very practical thing.
I was working with a youth group in Europe,
and they'd have these great,
long meetings where they would discuss things,
and they had a lot of trouble reaching consensus.
And I, and we were in a meeting room,
and I said, "Look, guys, let's put up on a flip chart,
you know, the ops, and the alternatives.
And we'd we'd list maybe 10 possible courses of action.
And then we, I taught them
what sort of jokingly became known
as the famous Larry Miller dot method.
And we had magic markers, right?
And I say, "Okay, everybody in the room,
you get three dots."
Get out of your seats,
take the magic marker, don't cheat.
You can only have three dots.
And by the, let's say the 10, or 20 possible items,
put your three favorites.
Put dots by the three items
that you think are the best decisions,
or the best courses of action,
whatever it is you're trying to reach consensus on.
And it, you know, it takes 10 minutes, they do that.
It's a good exercise, 'cause they get the blood flowing,
they get out of their seat.
And then, you know, when you look at all the dots,
it usually becomes apparent
that there are one, two, or three
favored courses of action.
So, and now sometimes you can do
more than one course of action, right?
So maybe we eliminate all
but the top three vote getters in there.
And then we do it again.
And we say, "Okay, now everybody, you only get one dot."
And you do it again,
and you come out with one
that gets the highest vote, and you say,
"Okay, is everybody willing to go along with that?"
And having gone through the exercise,
and everybody having had their say,
when people have had their say,
and they've been heard,
they're usually willing to go along.
So you do that, you have consensus,
and it can be, it can be very quick.
And it's a sort of a practical way of getting there.
I hope that's helpful to you.
Thanks a lot.
(bright upbeat music)

--- Principles of Conflict Resolution ---

In the next lecture, I'm gonna give
you a model of conflict resolution,
sort of steps, you know to go through.
And then in the following lectures,
I want to talk about the nature of conversation,
the culture of conversation, dialogue
and debate among your team members,
and how you can seek to create an effective culture
where people really listen to each other,
and that, again, is likely to reduce or resolve conflicts.
So here are some principles.
Here are nine principles that make sense to me.
I hope they make sense to you.
First of all, it's really useful to understand
that from whatever, wherever
people are in their perspective,
whatever their position is, they are right.
Yes, everybody is right.
I know that sort of sounds absurd.
But given their life experience,
given their experience in the workplace,
given what matters to them, given what they have learned,
what has paid off for them in the past, right,
what's gotten reinforced in the past,
given all their history, their position is correct.
Now, the other person and the other person may be you,
but the other person is also correct,
given their glasses that they're looking through,
which is their life experience
and the learning that they have gone through.
So the key to conflict resolution
is not to decide who's right and wrong.
You know, you have to get out of that mentality
of being a referee and saying he's out or he is not out.
No, that's not the game of conflict resolution.
The game of conflict resolution is to understand
that everybody's right, given their background,
and to help each party, which may be you
and the other person, understand the other person,
listen to the other other person,
have empathy for the other person,
and then try to reach a mutually agreeable solution,
which will only come if you first have that empathy,
if you first have that understanding
between the two parties.
So first, keep in mind that everybody's right,
given their history.
Avoid false dichotomies.
What are false dichotomies?
A dichotomy is black and white, left and right,
liberal and conservative in our political world, right.
And we have a bad habit of approaching a conflict
or a problem and taking a left or right,
black or white position, which is often false,
because often the truth is some combination
of ideas that is not necessarily left or right,
liberal or conservative, whatever the dichotomy is.
So watch, when you see a conflict,
think about whether or not the two parties
are falling into false dichotomies.
Often when you hear a conflict,
somebody has a conflict with you, it is very often the case
that their presenting position is not the real problem.
They're presenting what they're able to articulate,
they're presenting what they feel.
But your job as a conflict resolver, a mediator,
or a facilitator, your job is to go below the presenting
position to understanding what the real concern is.
And that requires your listening skills,
your empathy, reflecting, and all those things
that you you know how to do well now.
When people have a conflict, their immediate,
and it's sort of an animal impulse, It's our lower nature.
Our impulse is to prove that I'm right and you are wrong.
We want to win.
You know, we've been brought up seeking to win
and not be a loser, right.
I don't want to be a loser, I want to be a winner.
So we dig in and we go into argument.
You are wrong because, I'm right because.
Don't dig in, don't allow people to dig in.
Help them understand,
and you try to understand what their viewpoint is,
what their position is, and help them understand
the other person's viewpoint and position.
And don't engage in argument.
You know, listen for the sound of argument,
and try to change that conversation to,
what we'll talk about later is dialogue rather than debate,
where we're actually listening and learning
from each other rather than trying to win an argument.
Don't use exaggerated statements.
You know who's the worst at this
is spouses, husbands and wives.
You never pick up your socks.
You never clean the bathroom.
Yes, I did, you know, last October, I cleaned the bathroom.
We tend to try to win an argument or make our point by using
escalated, exaggerated statements.
Well, do those help resolve a conflict?
No, they actually create more conflict,
'cause then the other person becomes defensive,
and the other person says, you're wrong.
And in fact, you never.
I always.
Whenever somebody says always or never
in a conflict situation, they're wrong.
Okay, 'cause always and never are never correct,
if that makes sense.
So when you hear that, try to say never,
you know, he never does that?
John, is that right, do you never do that?
He says, yeah, it's only on Thursdays I don't do it.
Or whatever, so.
Avoid exaggerated statements.
When you are trying to mediate or resolve a conflict,
you are trying to be a unifier.
And what you want to do is seek points of unity.
And the points of unity are larger purpose.
You know, look guys, what are we both
really trying to accomplish?
What is our unified purpose?
You know, it's a little bit like,
we had an election here in the United States,
and you had very polarized positions.
And there was a lot of you never and I always, you know,
those kinds of dichotomous statements and positions.
But once that's over, you step back and you say,
wait a minute, what is our common purpose?
What is our common objective?
What can we be unified around?
And inevitably there are things
that we can and we should be unified around,
and we come together on those things.
And that's true in the workplace too.
If two team members are in conflict,
hey guys, let's back up and say
what is our real purpose here?
What are we both trying to achieve?
And as team members, we're both trying to achieve
doing well for our customer, isn't that right?
That is right, it should be right, that should be right.
And let's talk about how we can,
given this situation that we're in conflict over,
let's talk about what would best serve the customer.
Let's come together on that.
And out of that unifying process,
a solution very often comes.
Focus on where we're going, not where we have been,
and where we're going is that common purpose.
We can spend a lot of time in argument
about who did what, when, why, all in the past.
And almost none of that is gonna solve the problem.
What's gonna solve the problem is talking about the future,
talking about where we're going,
and what we need to do in the future.
Remember what Dr. Demming said,
that 95% of problems are in the process, not the person.
We tend to blame the person and not fix the process.
So as you're listening to a conflict,
ask yourself is the problem really here the person
or is the problem the process, the process of work,
the process we're engaged in.
And if you think about that, and you can help them,
the two parties, think about let's not focus on the person,
let's think about the process,
let's think about how to improve how we do things
so this isn't a problem in the future.
Hope that makes sense.
Look for win-wins.
You want to come out of any conflict situation
with mutual benefit, with mutual gain for both parties.
It's never a good conflict resolution to say, John,
you're the winner, and Sam, you're the loser.
You know, no, 'cause that will come back inevitably.
Think about how both parties, how the team
can be a winner in whatever that situation is.
I hope those nine principles make make sense to you.
Let's go on and let's just look at sort of a more
specific process to go through when resolving conflicts.
Thanks.

--- A Model of Conflict Resolution ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let go through a simple seven step process,
which you see here for resolving conflicts,
and, you know, when you have your next conflict,
you might even write these seven steps down
or print them out and have them in front of you
and think about them as you go through trying
to resolve the conflict.
The principles, the nine principles that we talked
about in the previous lecture are all embedded
in these steps.
And all your listening skills are embedded in these steps.
So step number one, find agreement.
You have your two parties there who are in disagreement.
And let's first define the problem.
Let's agree on what the problem is,
'cause very often, people don't see the problem
as the same problem.
They're actually arguing over something
that, in their minds, you know, it's like wars.
I was in a war.
I found that both participants in the war,
they're fighting different wars.
They're not fighting the same war.
So it's really helpful to understand
what war they're fighting.
You know, what is the problem as they see it?
When you think about how the two parties
see the conflict that they're in,
they may actually not be
in the same conflict, right?
Like two participants
in a war may not really be fighting the same war.
They have different ideas about why they're there.
So if you can understand what the problem is
from each perspective, and then
what are they in agreement about?
Now, they're probably both in agreement
that we wanna perform well.
We're both in agreement
with that we want the team to do well.
We're both in agreement that we want to serve
our customers well.
We're both in agreement
that we want improve the process, for example.
My guess is you can find agreement between the two parties
and that's always a good place to start.
Clarify exactly what the disagreement is.
And here's where you're gonna use
your reflective listening skills.
You're gonna listen to each person explain
what they see as the disagreement
or the conflict, the problem.
Let them hear each other, right?
Or if you're part of it, you're explaining how you see it
and then you're listening well
to how the other person sees that.
And you can't solve a problem
unless you understand what the disagreement is about.
So use your good listening skills.
Express empathy.
Now, I can't explain this, you know, well,
'cause there's sort of a magic,
there's sort of a chemistry
and expressing empathy is like oiling the gears, right?
When you understand my point of view,
when you understand my problem,
when you understand why I'm concerned
and not just understanding it factually,
but understanding how it feels,
understanding why it is a problem for me, why I'm concerned,
why I am hurt, you know, whatever the situation is,
the simple act of understanding my feelings
and my position relaxes me.
You know, very often people will argue harder and louder,
not because of the actual problem,
but because they just don't feel
like you're listening to me.
You're not understanding me.
So I sort of escalate the problem.
I escalate my tone of voice, my volume.
You know, how loud I am, until I hear you say
I can understand why that's a problem.
I can understand that's painful for you.
I can understand that that's frustrating for you.
Those statements, you know,
are very powerful part of conflict resolution.
Once you have understood why people feel the way they do
and what their positions are,
and what we're in agreement with,
now let's sort of be creative.
Let's kind of brainstorm alternatives, right?
Let's think about, you know, let's make a list
of the three, five, 10 possible solutions
that are not necessarily yours or yours, but are others.
You know, let's think about other solutions to the problem.
And when we sort of brainstorm different solutions
to a problem, we seek alternatives,
we may find an alternative we both agree
with and we can abandon our previous positions.
That's very, very common.
So just the act of brainstorming alternatives to get
to our shared goal where we both want to get to,
that helps to resolve a conflict.
Let's agree on criteria for a solution.
Does it have to be immediate?
Can it be long-term solution?
Does it just involve the two of us
or does it need to involve 10 other people?
You know, what are the criteria
for what a good solution to this conflict would look like?
Then try combining ideas.
You know, and again,
this sort of gets away from the false dichotomy idea.
Very often you can take the solution that one person has
and the solution that another person has and combine them.
They're not always either/or, right?
You know, I consulted with the school board
of an elementary school that my children were going
to some years ago, and they had a conflict,
you know, half of the staff of the school
and the board felt that the core purpose
of the school was to create a love
of learning in the children.
There was another camp that said, well, yeah, that's great,
but they have to be able to pass tests,
'cause if they're gonna go now to public high school
or another high school, they're gonna be tested.
And they have to know some math,
they have to know some history.
They have to know stuff, right,
'cause love of learning is not on a test,
you know, math is on a test.
And they had sort of created these camps
where they really felt like we had to choose
whether our core mission was to create love
of learning or knowledge.
And it was like a big battle in the school.
It may sound silly to you, but it wasn't silly to them.
And I just drew this, you know, matrix, you know,
being the management consultant that I am,
with love of learning on one side
and knowledge on the other side and said,
is it possible to go northeast?
Increasing love of learning,
at the same time increasing knowledge?
And I know that seems like a similar idea,
but for them it was a big breakthrough.
It was like, oh yeah, maybe we can figure out
how to teach in a way and conduct our classrooms
in a way where they are actually learning some math,
but doing it in a way that's fun.
And they're learning to love learning as opposed
to just learning to love math or learning math.
Any of that made sense?
So try combining, eliminating false dichotomies.
Be creative with new possible solutions.
Now you can fulfill your criteria with some combination
of the previous ideas that have been floated.
And then, you know, you reach consensus,
you ask those who are engaged in the conflict.
Can you both agree that we'll try the following?
And it's very good.
And this is sort of consistent with lean management.
Toyota production system.
Problem solving.
Every solution is an experiment, okay?
So we don't have to agree that a course
of action is the ultimate final end state course of action.
Let's agree to try this
and see what happens, see if it works.
And then let's come back
and let's try, you know, try something else,
modify it or agree that we just keep doing that.
So it's very, very helpful
in terms of reaching consensus to understand
that what we're really trying to agree on is a consensus
to move forward with an experiment.
And people are more likely to accept that than thinking
that this is the one final solution to a problem.
So I hope those seven steps make sense.
Try them out next time you have a conflict
and see how they work for you.
Thanks a lot.
(upbeat music)

--- Conflict Resolution: Debate or Dialogue? ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi again, friends, I wanna share a model of conversation
and I hope this will help you create healthy conversation
within your group, within your team.
And I hope it will help you overcome conflict
and avoid conflict on your team.
Let me suggest to you
that there are three Ds of conversation,
three types of conversations that go on among people.
One kind of conversation is debate.
And if you look at this diagram,
at the bottom, and it's intentionally at the bottom,
there are two corners that are you and me.
I wanna win, you wanna win, I have my position.
Think about an actual debate.
Think about whether you were in a high school debate club
or you've ever watched a debate, presidential debate,
any kind of debate.
When you go into a debate,
you prepare before the debate,
and you've prepared to prove that your position is right.
You know what your position is when you go into a debate,
you know that you're gonna argue for a position,
and the other person,
you know they're gonna argue against your position
and for their position.
So we're sort of cemented in our corners.
And in a debate, it's like an argument, right?
I am set out to prove that I am right,
I'm not trying to learn from you,
I'm not trying to adapt your ideas,
I'm trying to make you adapt mine,
or make the judges understand that my position
is the best position, that's a debate.
A discussion is a less owned, less engaged,
we're not so far into our corners,
we're somewhere in the middle,
where we're just thinking about what to do
and sharing information and so forth,
but we're not really acting as one,
we're not really unified,
but we're listening to each other to a degree, right?
We're having a healthy discussion.
Then at the top of the pyramid is what is called dialogue.
Now, we're never gonna always be in a state of dialogue,
but when we have an important matter to discuss on our team,
it is useful to try to engage in dialogue.
And in dialogue, we're thinking together,
we're trying to create a unified mind.
We're acting as we, I have given up my ego.
When you go into a debate in your position,
your ego is attached, you don't wanna be a loser,
you wanna be a winner, so your ego is attached,
as is the other person.
And that almost prevents learning,
prevents coming up with shared solution.
The degree to which we are focused on the team,
we, rather than my position,
we are then able to engage in dialogue.
And if you look at this, the purpose of conversation,
there's sort of a hierarchy,
where at the bottom is winning,
then you can sort of go to, well,
I'm just trying to convince, maybe lecturing,
teaching, sharing, trying to gain understanding,
really trying to learn from each other.
And then serving,
if you're really in a state of dialogue,
you don't care about what's for me, or winning,
ego is detached,
and you're really trying to achieve an outcome
that is a service leadership, a servant position,
of serving the group, serving the we,
it's truly a shared purpose that we're focused on.
And when we're in that mental state,
we're able to get to a place in terms of solving problems,
creating unity of the group that we're never able to achieve
if we're stuck in our corners.
So think about the nature of conversation.
And in the next lecture,
I wanna go through some behavioral characteristics,
how do we behave when we're in a debate culture
versus how do we behave when we're in a dialogue culture?
And I'm gonna ask you some questions about that,
and you can actually create a score.
You can see where you think your group is.
Hope that helps, thanks.
(upbeat music)

That's inquiring, right?
Why do you feel that way? Tell me what's behind that idea.

That's always a dangerous thing.
So again, give yourself, you know,

Suspending judgment versus speed of judgment.

In reality, in the knowledge world that we live in,

Where if you're quick to judge, you know,

Silence...

Okay, four,
one right way versus open to alternatives.
Again, in debate, you know, I know the right answer,

and I'm gonna convince you that that's the one right way.
Well, that blocks off learning, doesn't it?
I mean, you can't be in a learning mode

In a dialogue culture,

We think they know what their idea's about,

It really blocks learning by that categorizing

--- Developing Your Team Scorecard ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi, friends.
Welcome back.
I want to talk about scorekeeping,
visual display, setting objectives, goals,
all those things around numbers in the workplace, right?
That's what scorekeeping is about,
and that's what visual display is about,
and what objective setting, goal setting are all about.
Now, remember our three circles,
and you might say, well, where are we on those?
We've covered the people,
we've done a lot in terms of developing the team,
and we're moving into the process section.
But the reality is, this is sort of
in the overlap because when you create scorekeeping,
of course you're doing that with your team.
I want you to engage your team
in deciding what their score board, scorecards,
visual display should be.
But what are you keeping score on?
I mean, you're not keeping score on the weather.
You're keeping score on the work process, on the workflow,
the output of the work, the cycle time of the work,
the defects that occur in the work process.
So scorekeeping and objective setting should all be
about the work process as well.
So we're sort of in that, you know,
overlap period between the two circles of team and process.
And, of course, everything we learned
about managing people all fits in there too, right?
Now, you remember when we talked about team formation,
we said that teams should be formed around a process.
Teams should know what their process is,
they should know what process they own,
and they should be focused on improving that process.
And you cannot improve the process
unless you know how the process is performing,
which means scorekeeping, of course.
So we wanna engage in continuous improvement, right?
We wanna move up the mountain,
our little mountain climber here, moving up the mountain.
And we're working on how people behave,
the culture of the organization, human performance.
But at the same time, we're monitoring the data,
we're monitoring the trends in the data,
and we're setting targets.
The targets we're setting are numerical targets
to improve process performance.
And that's what we should be keeping score on.
Team scores should be integrated vertically
from top to bottom.
And this is, you know, a basic of objective setting.
You don't set objectives
or decide on performance independent

I mean, just imagine, for example,

--- The Balanced Scorecard ---

(bright upbeat music)
(bright upbeat music continues)
-: Okay, let's talk about a Balanced Scorecard.
Now, this idea has become very popular
in business for a good reason.
Kaplan and Norton wrote a book called,
"The Balanced Scorecard."
You can get that if you wanna dig into it deeply,
but it's not a complicated thing.
And they didn't prescribe exactly
what your scoreboard should be
because they don't know your work.
Just like, I don't know your work,
but the idea, let me give you the basic idea
and then suggest a way to go
about creating a balanced scorecard.
This really got started with two problems.
One problem is there were companies
that all they looked at was money.
All they looked at was finances.
And everything that they cared about,
the only measures that were important
in their minds were financial measures,
and quality declined, and productivity may have declined.
They were so busy.
If you only focus on financial measures,
what you tend to do is cut cost.
Well, if all you do is cut cost, you're gonna hurt quality,
and you're gonna hurt your customer satisfaction.
No, you have to be focused not just on finances,
although you do have to be focused on finances,
but you also have to be focused
on customer satisfaction and quality.
So the idea of a balanced scorecard,
and by the way, there was also
a big productivity movement back in the eighties,
I guess, there was American Productivity Center
and lots of books on productivity improvement.
And again, if everybody's just focused on, you know,
productivity is the ratio of input to output, okay?
Input of costs and output in terms of dollars
and widgets or a number of cars produced per person hour,
that's a productivity measure.
Or a number of cars produced per amount of space.
That's another productivity measure.
Different ways of computing productivity,
but it's always a ratio, right?
Well, that's good.
That's an important way of measuring performance,
but it doesn't necessarily produce quality.
It doesn't necessarily produce customer satisfaction, right?
So the idea of a balanced scorecard was literally
to have a balance of measures that equal total performance,
and that's what you want.
You want total performance.
Just a funny thing if you're interested,
go on YouTube and look up, "I Love Lucy."
If you remember, "I Love Lucy,"
you'll see on YouTube, a really funny skit.
Anyway, Lucy and her friend go decide to get a real job
as opposed to just being housewives,
and they get a job in a chocolate factory. (chuckles)
And the supervisor, all she cares about obviously,
is the speed of the line.
And she's speeding up the line,
and they're hiding the candies.
And it's just a sort of a humorous example
of what happens if you only focus on one measure.
So here's a balanced scorecard.
This is a sort of a normal, if you will,
balanced scorecard model.
In the middle, there's the idea of what is our strategy?
What are we really trying to achieve in the long term?
And then there are usually four quadrants
of the balanced scorecard, four components.
One, being customer satisfaction, quality.
Customer satisfaction and quality go together.
Another is measure of our business process, our workflow.
We'll come back and talk about that.
Financial measures and then learning,
and the development of our people.
If you're focused on learning and development,
you'll tend to improve the others.
If you're focused on the others,
but not thinking about learning and development,
in the long term, you're gonna trouble succeeding
in the others.
So I want you to consult
with your team on the balanced scorecard,
and I want you to explain the four elements.
You can put up a visual of those four elements.
And I would like you to consult with the team
on what they think are good measures
that fall into each of those categories.
And let's just go through each
of them in a little more depth.
You know, customer or client satisfaction.
How do we know?
And just think.
Ask yourself, when was the time you knew
that your customers were delighted,
when they were really happy?
How did they know to be happy?
What was it that made them happy?
Was it different specifications that they got in the,
was it how you delivered it?
Was it in the human interaction?
That's very often the case, how you serve them.
Maybe that's it.
And maybe, there's something we can measure there.
Think about all the reasons why they may
be either happy or unhappy.
And if you don't know, ask them.
They will tell you. (chuckles)
It's always the best way to know what leads
to customer satisfaction is to ask the customer.
So why don't you try that and think about
how you might measure that.
We're gonna talk much more
about our process and how to improve our process,
but first, we have to measure it.
We know we have that input, output, SIPOC, right?
You got that.
And now there's cycle time, time through the flow
from the time stuff comes in to the time stuff goes out.
That's cycle time.
Reliability. How reliable are we
in producing the product consistently?
You know, that's reliability.
Variability, reducing variability.
We'll talk about variability more later,
but we should be looking at our graph.
We should be graphing all data,
and we should look at that variability.
And when does it vary?
You know, why does it vary?
That is usually consistent
with what your customers
might think is reliable performance.
Productivity, we've talked about that.
You know, every process can be measured in terms
of its productivity, the amount of people,
the amount of space, the amount of money divided
by the output, right?
That's productivity.
Variability. I talked about that.
How does the process vary and when does it vary?
How many improvements do we make in the process?
How often do we improve the process?
That's innovation.
That's a form of innovation.
There's innovation in both product or service,
and then there's an innovation
in producing it or delivering it, right?
Process innovation. Product and process innovation.
When you think about the whole Toyota production system,
lean management business, it's all about process.
It's not product. It's about innovation
and making continuous improvements

So you can measure that.
You can say, how many improvements have we made
in the last week, month, year?
When you think about process measures,
when you think about the flow,
there are end of process measures.
You know, things coming out the other end.
There's feedback from the customer
that can tell you about your process.
There's behavior within the process.
And you can look at each process step, right?
And say, what's the result of that process step?
And can you measure the result of a single process step?
If you have eight people working in a team
and there's a flow around a work cell,
you can measure each function
on the work cell by the output to the next station,
if you understand what that means.
That's what each of those little arrows represent.
So think about all the different process measures.
Financial results. You're not a manager,
unless you know the financial numbers.
You may think you're a manager,
but you're not really a manager,
unless you know the financial numbers.
Revenue, costs, and return on assets.
You employ assets. What's the return on those assets?
It's your job to increase return on assets.
It's your job to hold down cost,
get the most you can done with the least cost.
And it's your job, for most managers,
to increase revenue or at least not reduce revenue, right?
So you and your team, whether those are,
it's a very simplistic way of looking at it,
but I want you to think about with your team
what financial measures you can keep score of.
And then learning and development.
You know, I worked with a Merck plant,

the total number of managers who were all going through it.
That was a measure
of learning development that they employed.
But you can think of other demonstrating skills,
demonstrating knowledge, going through learning courses,
how many people are multi-skilled?
If you have a team where there are eight different functions
on the team, if you look at each person,
certainly, they can do one function, I hope. (chuckles)
But how many other functions can they do?
Well, in the ideal world,
the answer would be eight times eight, right?
But you're probably not there.
You're probably not at the point where every employee
on the team can do every job.
But that's a measure of multi-skilled on a team,
so think about that.
And here's just a real quick process
for establishing a balanced scorecard.
Define the measures,
establish baseline data for each measure,
graph the baseline data.
You're going to, oh, then set targets, right?
Now set measurable, but reasonable targets.
You don't set a target for a whole year.
You set a target for this month, right?
Short-term targets. We're gonna walk up the staircase,
and we're gonna go through a plan, do, check, act.
We'll talk about that very shortly.
We're gonna go use problem-solving methods to improve
from the baseline, but you can't really improve,
unless you know what the baseline is.
You won't know what you're doing, right?
So get your baseline data.
We're gonna engage in problem-solving with your team.
Then you're gonna evaluate the results,
and you're gonna celebrate success.
That's basic use of the balanced scorecard.
I hope that makes sense.
I know, it's not complicated.
Don't make it complicated.
Don't take too much time with it.
Accept the fact that whatever the scorecard
you come up with is your best idea today.
Everything's an experiment.
Everything we do in management is an experiment,
and you'll improve it over time.
You and your team will improve it over time.

--- Targets, Goals and Objectives ---

(cheerful music)
-: Hi, friends.
I'd like to talk in this session
about goal setting, setting targets,
objective setting, MBO,
all those things of setting expectation and accountability.
And this is an old story in management,
and we've learned a lot about it over the years.
Now, some people just reject the idea
of setting goals and objectives completely,
and some people think it's just a very simple process
of give 'em an objective and hold 'em accountable.
Life should be so simple, right?
It's not.
It's a little more complicated than that,
but there is no question
that setting targets, goals, or objectives
is an absolutely essential part of managing others,
and more importantly, managing yourself.
We all respond to goal setting.
You know, when I was a young man,
I began to read for some reason
books about young men becoming successful,
the Horatio Alger stories.
Still probably pretty great stories,
and they're all the rags to riches stories.
And then W. Clement Stone and Dale Carnegie,
and other luminaries of the motivation field.
And what they all had in common
was the idea of some usually young man, some poor young man,
finding a model, finding somebody that they said,
"Wow, if only someday I could be like that,"
and creating a goal, an image in their mind,
and setting out to climb the mountain, if you will.
They had a goal, but they also had the ability
to go through baby steps,
to look at what they had to do in the short term
and overcome that sequence of goals that got you there.
Now, that's a universal process.
We all should be doing that in our lives.
I think everybody should have a big dream,
and it doesn't have to be materialistic.
It doesn't have to be to have the big house on the hill.
It can be something more spiritual,
something of a higher value or more purposeful, if you want.
And there's nothing wrong
with having several different big dreams,
but the important thing is that we operationalize the dream,
that we break that dream down into behavior,
into things we're gonna do today and tomorrow and next week,
and we walk towards the mountain.
We gotta climb the mountain.
You can't dream about
getting to the top of the mountain,
and not do anything.
It's all about the doing that gets you there.
And it's right to have a goal, a big goal,
but it's also right to break that down
and start doing the baby steps to get you there.
You know, when I was beginning my career,
and I just, more by accident than anything else,

Now, so what's the lesson of that?

--- MBO & Self-Control ---

(bright music)
-: I wanna go back to this whole thing
of management by objectives.
And if you've been part of the quality movement,
you know that Dr.Deming
railed against management by objectives.
I mean, one of his 14 points was to eliminate
management by objectives.
Now, he was half right, he was not all right about this.
And the reason he was so opposed to management by objectives
was because of how he saw it had been employed
and frankly, -- in corporations.
Hold that thought.
Now I wanna go back to 1954 before you were born probably,
go back to 1954 and a book was published
by then a young professor named Peter Drucker
called The Practice of Management.
And there was a chapter in the practice of management
that really was the root
of the whole management by objectives thing.
And do you know what the title of that chapter was?
It was Management by Objectives and Self-Control.
Management by Objectives and Self-Control, very important.
Peter Drucker's original conception of MBO,
Management by Objectives was that people
would set objectives and it would impose on themself
a form of self-control.
Now I want you to think Saturday morning
you're going golfing and you go, you know,
you've played this course before.
And if you don't play golf,
actually I don't play golf either.
But just think about the metaphor,
think about the example and apply it to whatever you do do.
But at Saturday morning, you're going to play on this course
that you've played before.
And you know the best you've ever shot
on this course was 95,
took you 95 shots to go around the 18 holes.
Probably on average, maybe a hundred.
But let's just say your best round ever was 95.
Saturday morning, you go to put the ball down on the tee
to tee off.
What are you thinking?
What do you say to yourself?
You say to yourself, I'm gonna beat 95.
I'm gonna do it 94, 93, 92, something like that.
Nobody, nobody says knowing that their best score was 95,
you know, today I'm gonna shoot 105.
Does anybody do that?
Nobody does that.
There is something innate in us
that when we know our performance
we try to beat it, we try to do better.
And that's objective setting.
We're setting an objective for ourself, right?
And that's the game.
I mean, the game is to beat the goal that we set,
to beat our own performance, to do better
than we've done before.
We love doing that.
And it doesn't matter whether it's golf
or playing the guitar or tennis
or anything else, running, anything else you may do.
Now, I want you to imagine we're about to put
the ball down on the tee and there's a guy over on the side,
he's not on the green, he's sort of off the green.
And he says, ah, Larry, today I want you to shoot a 92.
Let me know how it goes.
What, you want me to shoot a 92?
Who are you?
Come on.
It's my supervisor.
This just became work.
The fun of the game just got destroyed and it got destroyed
because I'm not setting the objective myself.
It's not management by objectives and self-control.
Now it's management by objectives
and somebody else trying to control me.
And the feeling of that and the motivation that's produced
by that is completely different.
If you understand that,
you understand effective setting objectives
and effective setting objectives is to engage somebody
or the team with the data in understanding the data
and understanding how they're currently performing
and saying, what do you think we can do?

--- The Skills of Brainstorming ---

First of all, the most important thing is to understand
why you're doing brainstorming and what brainstorming is.
And brainstorming is not deciding anything.
Separate deciding, judging what's good, what's bad,
you know, all the other things on the A three,
all the other boxes, forget about them.
When you're brainstorming, the idea is to be creative,
and to come up with things that you wouldn't
otherwise have come up with.

or chemistry in brainstorming.
And the chemistry is this.
When you brainstorm, you want people
to say wild and crazy things.

in saying wild and crazy things?
That's a waste of time, isn't it?
No, because one wild and crazy idea

and that next idea may stimulate
something in somebody else's brain.

might be the really great idea.
So that's sort of the magic, that's sort of the chemistry.
But what you have to do in order to get there
is you have to suspend judgment.
And this is, if you're facilitating brainstorming,

You tell the team, guys, you know,
we're gonna generate ideas,

We can't do that.

Whether people start making judgements,
and if they start making judgements, you know,
you've gotta say, time out guys,
we're not judging now, we're brainstorming, okay.
We're just trying to generate ideas
and build on each other's ideas, right.
To see how creative we can be.
So that's the real chemistry,
and that's the discipline,
the idea is to come up with a maximum number of ideas,
to generate new and creative ideas.

all team members the opportunity.

I might never talk talkers in the room.

Some people you have to invite.

--- What Are Processes and Capabilities? ---

(soft music)
-: Hi again, friends.
This whole section, the last section of the course,
I know you're happy to be there,
is on process management, process improvement.
And I want you to just think
about the flow of the three circles here.
And I want to introduce the word capability.
And the purpose of this course has been to
improve your capability to lead, develop,
manage individuals, teams, and processes, right?
And if you think about that process,
first of all everything we learned
in the managing people section,
enabled you to manage teams well.
If you are able to manage teams well,
teams own and manage a work process.
So if your team is highly capable,
if you're a highly capable team leader,
you have highly capable individuals that you developed well,
now they're managing their work process.
Now they're managing their workflow.
And if you think about the idea of capability
in an organization, an organization is capable to the degree
that each process within the organization is capable.
So you, as a manager of a team and a process,
you are a brick on the building, if you will.
You are managing one
of those processes that enable the organization to succeed
enable the organization to be capable.
What is a process?
You know, we've used this term over and over again.
Let's make sure we understand it
'cause we're gonna proceed to map it
and eliminate waste from it.
And improve it.
A process is a set of activities,
that you could view those activities separately
and individually but those activities connect to each other.
They have a flow across the activities that
produces an outcome that serves a customer.
Does that make sense?
Easy example, we're a restaurant.
We're making meals and serving customers, right?
There are a whole bunch of steps in the process
buying the incoming food, right?
Buying the lettuce and the meat and the so forth.
And you can follow through the imagine the process
till the customer's leaving the restaurant feeling filled
and happy about their meal.
That's a process, right?
With inputs and outputs and so forth.
But there are a set of discreet activities.
And every discreet activity has to be performed well
in order for the whole chain.
Every activity is a link on a chain.
Put them together, you have a process.
Now what are capabilities and capabilities reside
within processes, capable individuals make processes capable
and capable processes make the organization capable
if that makes sense.
And there are both technical and human capabilities,
if you think about it for a second.
Just imagine we're racing cars, right?
And I'm Louis Hamilton
or Nico Rossberg Formula One World champions.
I'm a great driver

Okay? An old Volkswagen Beetle.

Probably you'll be able to beat me.
'cause the moment we're side by side and you put your foot

And you'll be able to go
around the corners faster and so forth.
Now you could, you could mess it up
but you're probably gonna beat me.
Not because you're more capable
but because the car, the technical thing.
And within that car, there are a whole set of processes.
An engine contains a process
or it's one big process, right?
A fuel coming in and horsepower going out,
or torque going out.
That's a process, as there is a process
in an individual driver having human capabilities
and what he or she does to drive the car.
So when we make our organization capable
we have to make our processes capable.
And when we think about our processes, we have to think
about both the technical and the human side of capabilities.
So here sort of summarizes what capabilities are.
They're the ability of people, processes, and technology,
the social and the technical, to perform
in a manner that satisfies customers
and achieves our strategy.
We want to satisfy our customers

They do not pay you for how well you develop your people,

What do they pay for?

Now, there are also enabling processes.
The human resource process,

how good they are,

We don't have manufacturing

whatever the core process is, right?
So it's important to understand who's the customer

Now, here's the thing about change

They're all moving, they're not standing still.

We have to constantly be sensing what's going
on in the outer world and saying,
how can that create advantage for us?

how could that disadvantage us?

--- Eliminating Waste from Your Process ---

(bright music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Now that you know your customer requirements,
now that you've mapped your process,
you've looked at cycle time,
now it's time to find and eliminate waste.
And I want you to understand waste,
because it's an important concept,
particularly if you've read about Lean manufacturing
or Lean healthcare.
This term, Lean, it all comes from Toyota Production System.
And it's worth understanding
where Toyota Production System began,
because it illustrates exactly what waste is.
First of all, waste is not people.
People are not waste.
So when you think about eliminating waste,
you ought not be thinking about eliminating people.
You should be thinking about eliminating
the unnecessary things that people do,
usually not by their own fault,
but because of the nature of the process.
Let's go to Toyota about 50 years ago,
and there was a man named Shigeo Shingo,
who was the manufacturing or engineering head at Toyota,
and he observed a stamping press.
And you have to picture a stamping press.
It's a big thing.
A flat sheet of sheet metal goes in,
and the stamping press comes down,
and by stamping it, think of a cookie cutter.
You know, when you're making Christmas cookies,
little Santa Clauses or Christmas trees or something,
you stamp out from the cookie dough
the shape of the cookie you want, right?
Well, a stamping press is exactly the same thing,
just on a much larger scale and with metal.
It stamps out the shape of a left front fender
or a right front fender or the bumper,
or some other shape that's part of your car.
Well, Shigeo Shingo watched the stamping press in operation
and the team of people working around the stamping press.
Now, he had been to the River Rouge plant of Ford
in Dearborn, Michigan.
This was in the old days of Ford, not today's days of Ford.
It's a big difference.
But in the old days of Ford,
the machine operators operating a stamping press,
there's a die.
Let me just back up one second.
There's a die that,
like think of the different cookie cutters, right?
The different shapes.
Well, the die is what is the shape,
and the stamping press is the big hydraulic thing
that pushes it down,
and you change dies for different parts.
I'm sure that makes sense, like different shapes of cookies.
And at Ford, when he observed the stamping press operation,
the press operators were specialized.
Remember this was sort of part of the mass production model
where every worker had very specialized jobs,
and unions, for some reason, bought into this whole idea
that everybody should have a very specialized job,
and you only do your own work.
You don't do what somebody else does.
And the press operators did not change the dies.
That was a different department in the plant.
There were specialized die change workers,
die change supervisor, die change department manager,
and it took on average about 24 hours to get a die changed.
Now, if it's gonna take 24 hours to change dies,
how many parts are you gonna make before changing a die?
Are you gonna make 10 parts?
I mean, the press comes down, boom, boom, stamped, right?
You're gonna just do 10 of 'em,
and then wait 24 hours for the dye to be changed?
Of course not.
You're gonna have a long run, right?
Because you don't wanna wait.
You don't want the machine standing around waiting
for 24 hours while you're trying to change the die.
Now if you're gonna have a long production run,
you have to put this stuff somewhere, right?
So it would, you know, you'd go on a pallet, let's say,
and the forklift would come
and would drive it into the warehouse,
and then on the assembly line, when they needed that part,
another forklift would go to the warehouse
and bring a pallet of them and put them there.
So you had to have the forklifts,
you had a forklift drivers, you had to have a warehouse,
you had to have all that stuff
that goes along with making a lot of them, okay?
Now, Shingo thought, you know,
but what if we could change dies in a minute
rather than 24 hours?
And they'd go, "Well, that's insane.
I mean, it takes more than a minute
to call the department that changes dies."
And he said,
"Yeah, but what if you just eliminated
that whole department?"
He played a lot of what-ifs, right?
And he talked to the workers.
This is critical.
He talked to the workers operating the press,
and he empowered them, he engaged them,
he respected them, important words,
and he asked them, he showed them the data.
He showed them, remember Mary, you know, in my textile mill?
Well, Shingo showed them the data
of what he considered the actual time of production.
Now, for one part, if you watch the workers work,
they're working hard, but they're lifting this,
and they're moving this, and they're...
And they're moving it in,
and then they're taking it out, and they're lifting it here,
and there's all that time.
But Shingo said the actual value-adding time
is just that time, boom, boom,
when the press stamps the part.
Everything else, he said, is waste.
Now, this is a very strict definition
of what is value-adding and what is waste.
'Cause if you ask the workers, they say,
"What are you talking about?
I have to lift this.
I have to move this.
I have to...
You know, I have to do all these things.
That's part of the job.
That's not waste."
Well, yeah, they have to do those things,
'cause that's the way the process is today,
but much of that stuff isn't value-adding.
So he started to ask them, "Well, what could you do?"
Well, first of all, you know,
if we had a tray where we had the sheet metal,
I'd have to walk over there and get a piece of sheet metal.
The sheet metal was stacked here,
and it was sort of on a spring-loaded platform,
where as I took one off, it would rise up,
and I could just slide it in, you know?
Or maybe it would automatically slide in, right?
I wouldn't have to go get it,
but they would just go, boom, boom, boom, boom,
sort of like that.
It would save me a lot of time and a lot of effort.
It's an idea.
Maybe we could do that.
Long story short, very long story,
a 40-year story of continuous improvement,
continuously seeking to eliminate non-value-adding time.
The last time I was in Marysville, Ohio,
and it's been a while, at the Honda plant,
they have what they called,
said they told me was the world's largest stamping press.
I don't know whether that's still true or not,
but that's what they said at the time.
And there was a flip chart next to the press,
and it had written on it just with a magic marker, 0.54.
Do you know what the 0.54 was?
It was 54 seconds to change dies
on the world's largest stamping press.
From 24 hours 50 years earlier at Ford,
to today, less than a minute die changeover.
Now that's the elimination of waste,
and that's where the concept comes from.
But it's not thinking about, you know,
I'm just eliminating costs, I'm eliminating salaries.
That's cut out a worker.
That's the old management consulting phony way
of eliminating waste or improving operations,
which didn't really improve operations.
It often resulted in worse operations and worse quality.
So that was the beginning of the Toyota Production System
or what we call now Lean management,
and it doesn't come from just sort of hammering the thing.
It comes from thinking,
and it comes from respecting the workers
who are the best observers
of how that work process gets done.
So let's look at the different kinds of waste
that Toyota talked about,
and maybe these kind of wastes are relevant to your work,
and maybe they're not.
Maybe you can think of the different forms of waste
that occur in your work setting.
And I'm gonna talk about management waste, by the way,
in the next lecture, 'cause there is that too, believe me.
But one form of waste.
There are seven forms of waste,
and there are Japanese terms for each of these
that some consultants like to throw around,
but I think that's just annoying to me.
But anyway, here are the seven forms of waste.
First one is excess inventory,
which means excess space in the warehouse,
and it also means that if you have 1,000 of them stacked up
versus, you know, a two-hour pile
that I talked about at Honda,
you're likely to have more defects.
When you discover a defect, you got a lot of garbage,
rather than just a little bit of garbage.
So the excess inventory has several forms of cost.
There's cost of space,
there's the actual cost of the stuff
that you have in the warehouse that you paid for,
but there's also cost in terms of defects.
Excess motion.
The guy having to walk over there
versus having the thing just lift up automatically.
You eliminated excess motion.
Think about motion.
Think about whether things are organized in a way,
and this is part of the 5S idea
of creating an orderly workspace
where tools are where they belong
and we know where they belong, and we can see them,
and they're neat and they're orderly,
so when I need one, I know exactly where to go to get it.
I don't waste any time.
I don't waste any motion.
Transportation.
The further we have to transport things,
and that's often within the facility.
It's that forklift moving stuff around.
Maybe we organize the line of work,
so the people doing work are right next to each other.
They can talk to each other.
They can move things instantly.
They don't have to walk around, wander around.
You know, we often organize things
in a way that maximize the need
for transportation, moving stuff.
Defects.
Whenever we have a defect,
and this is this is the cost of quality
or the cost of bad quality.
When we have a defect,
that's something we both wasted the materials involved.
We wasted the time of people making something
that can't be used,
but we also create rework.
You know, if I make one of these,
and it doesn't work, it's bad,
I have to make another one, right?
So there's a cost of redoing, rework,
and unfortunately people have become habituated to defects.
You know, this is the old story of US auto companies.
The workers in the plants were so disaffected,
disconnected from being respected in the work setting
that they would just pass defects on, not caring about it.
Well, that's the ultimate crime
and that's the ultimate failure of a system of work,
because the system of work must be such
that every employee on your team and in your system
cares deeply about any defect
and can stop the work to correct the defect.
Waiting time, anytime somebody has to stand.
You know, I love the story
where the leader of an American hospital went to Japan
and he explained all the waiting rooms.
I love it.
In a hospital, we have waiting rooms.
You have to wait here, you have to wait there,
you have to wait there, you have to wait.
And in the hospital complex,
they had 50-something waiting rooms,
and the Japanese executive just looked at him and said,
"Aren't you ashamed?"
And they said, "Yeah, actually,
when I think about it, I really am ashamed."
When people are sick, we should not keep them waiting.
But also in our production processes,
we should not have waiting time.
Overproduction.
You know what overproduction produces?
It produces stuff standing still,
stuff waiting, if you will,
but if we make too much,
and this is the idea in a Toyota Production System
of tack time, which is the idea of pull through, right?
Where you only make what's being demanded by customers
with the minimum amount of buffer,
work in process in between.
But the beat of the drum
that generates the production process
is the customer, right?
And it's a pull system rather than a push system.
US auto companies used to work on a push system.
We would make a ton of them.
We send 'em out to the dealers,
and we pray they can sell 'em.
They can't sell 'em, we give 'em incentives.
We reduce the price, right?
As opposed to actually watching what customers are buying,
and then producing that color, that model,
you know, that set of options in a car
based on the pull of the customer.
That reduces overproduction.
Excess processing time.
Anytime we can speed an operation,
the actual work flow by more skilled workers.
Very often, workers don't have the skill
or workers don't care,
or workers are not engaged in continuous improvement,
and we increase processing time.
So those are the seven forms of waste.
Look at your process, your process flow, and search,
search for the seven forms of waste.
Walk through the process.
Go and see, go to the gemba.
Go where the work is, watch it.
See if you can find those forms of waste.
Look at these two sheets.
First look at...
And I've attached these as an exercise to this section.
This is a waste elimination sheet,
and the next one is a waste improvement sheet.
List the steps in the process.
List the cycle time.
List the real work time, the value-added cycle time
versus the actual cycle time and the percent.
And go through and analyze it
and find ways that you can eliminate waste in the process.
Do that exercise with your team.
I think you'll find it enormously valuable,
and I'm positive you'll be able to find waste
in your process, whatever your process is.
Thanks.
