--- Eliminating Management Waste ---

(gentle upbeat music)
-: Hi again, friends.
Let's talk about management waste.
You know, just like we have leader standard work
because the same principles we're gonna apply
to our employees, we should be applying to ourself.
And just as we should think that, you know, every worker,
every employee, team member has standard work,
we should also have standard work.
Well, similarly, we're gonna look for waste
on the first level, we should be looking for how
we waste time or effort or money, different forms of waste
that are characteristic of managers.
And you know, over the last 40 plus years,
I've worked with a lot of senior leadership teams
in a lot of corporations.
And I am telling you that the forms of waste
at the senior levels, at management levels produce more cost
and more problems for the organization
than that at the bottom.
But they think because while I've been promoted up,
I must be above that.
I must somehow be exempt from creating waste.
Wrong!
Managers can create huge amounts of waste.
Believe it or not, I worked with an oil company
that spent a billion dollars there, rounded off, right,
thereabouts, drilling for oil in a central African country.
And they found it.
But then when they started thinking about how to produce it,
how to transport it, there was no pipeline
and to get to a port you had to go through on either side,
a country with which this country was at war,
meaning you couldn't, so you couldn't get it out.
And they'd spent a billion dollars finding it.
Well, hallelujah, they found it
but it was waste because you couldn't produce it.
Now, you know, you'd think
they would've thought of that, right?
But they didn't, they didn't think ahead,
they didn't strategize.
So there is waste, believe me at senior levels
in the organization.
Now, let's just list, I have six forms of management waste
and you know, you can think about which ones apply
to your organization and you can seek
to reduce them, I hope.
First one is sucking decisions up from those people
who are actually expert in what needs to be decided.
We have a tendency in our organizations to desire
to make decisions that ought to be made at a lower level.
This is what Dr. Deming called tampering.
Management should stop tampering with the work of others.
Get outta the way and let the people, empower the people,
train the people.
At the first level, the best decision making
is at the lowest possible level, the level of competence.
Because very often we suck decisions up to a level
of incompetence where they don't know how to do
what they do down there.
So let's make decisions at the right level,
which means empowering people at the lowest possible level.
That eliminates a lot of waste.
Displaying contradictory models.
Now, let me say this in the strongest possible terms.
I've led change processes
in many dozens of major corporations,
and one of the first things I tell the leadership team,
if you wanna work with me,
and I may not be the right consultant for you,
but if you wanna work with me, I have a requirement.
And that requirement is that you provide leadership.
And I want to tell you what leadership means.
Leadership means you doing the same things
we're gonna ask teams to do down at the lower levels
because they're going to look at you
and they're gonna model your behavior.
So if you are behaving in a contrary way,
the effort cannot succeed at lower levels.
So will you provide that leadership?
They usually say yes because will you provide leadership?
If you think you're a leader, you usually say yes to that.
But I also warn them that I'm gonna let them
know when they're not.
And I then take them through learning
how to do plan, do, check, act,
learning how to develop a team scorecard,
learning how to identify and map their work processes.
And even at the senior level, they have work processes.
So modeling is absolutely critical.
And when you present contrary models,
you're creating wasted effort at every level
in the organization.
Okay, not that I have strong feelings about that.
The failure to define and manage your own processes.
Well that's, that goes along with that, doesn't it?
Modeling good process management at management levels.
The failure of decision making.
Now, this is, again, you think, well,
you become a senior manager now you ought to know
how to make decisions well.
Well, very often decisions get made at management levels
that are not fact-based.
They don't play Sergeant Friday
they don't gather the facts,
they don't look at the data and make fact-based decisions.
It's very common and that creates waste.
We waste space and resources.
When Fiat bought Chrysler, they went
to the Chrysler building in Detroit
and there was a whole floor of the building
that was the executive boardroom floor.
Took a whole floor for a few people.
Why?
And what they did, they eliminated that whole floor.
When I worked at Honda, Mr. Irimajiri,
the president, has a desk that looks like
everybody else's desk, sits out in an open office area,
has no private offices.
Most private offices are waste.
They enable you to not have to think about
what the real value adding people are doing.
Get out there, get out where the work gets done.
Eliminate all that phony, celebratory space that you create
for yourself that makes yourself feel like you're important.
Well, that's all nonsense.
That's not how high performing organizations
or high performing managers function.
So wasted, that's wasted space and resources.
The failure of trust.
When we create distrust,
if I tell you something that is not true,
and it has happened in corporations where leaders
have said things that people then find out are not true,
people then tend not to follow.
They tend not to be dedicated.
They tend to not be willing to sacrifice
for that which they do not trust.
So creating trust, creating social capital as it is called
is an asset in every organization
and destroying social capital, destroying trust.
You destroy people's willingness to follow.
And that is a form of waste.
Think about those forms of waste in your own organization.
Thanks.
(gentle upbeat music)

--- Reducing Variances and Variability ---

(gentle music)
-: Hi again, folks.
Process variability and variances create waste.
Let's look at what it means,
what a variance means and what variability means,
and how we can seek to reduce those.
'Cause if we reduce those,
we improve the quality, the effectiveness of our processes.
Now a variance, just let me define that term.
And it's not so much a technical,
you know, it's not a statistical term.
There is statistical variability,
we'll talk about that in a minute.
But there is the general term of variances
as used in process improvement.
And a variance really is anything
that is operating different or performing differently
than it should perform.
The gap between the current performance,
the current behavior,
and the optimum behavior is a variance.
It varies from, right?
That's a gap analysis, if you will,
I guess you could call it that also.
So we wanna identify and reduce variances.
And here are six types of variances.
There may be variances from specifications,
you know, you're making a widget
and it's supposed to be this long,
but it's this long or it's this long, you know,
it varies from exactly what the specifications should be.
And many of the things we make have to fit something.
They may have to fit a computer.
They may have to fit,
you know, some other software program.
And if the specifications of a piece of software
don't match that to which they must fit,
that's basically a defect, right?
I mean, that's a variance in specifications.
There's variance in cycle time.
We've talked a lot about that.
And you understand the difference between
the value adding cycle time
and the actual cycle time, right?
Well, that gap is a variance
and there's always a variance there
in virtually any process at all.
There's variance from customer requirements, right?
The way we do things today,
I mean our customer's actually 100% delighted,
probably not, right?
There's some gap, there's some variance
between what the customer ideally would like
and what the current state is.
There's variance from cost of production
or service delivery.
Now, you know, we have to be cost-conscious,
because every work system there's a social system,
a technical system, and an economic system.
And we have to attend both to the technical system
like specifications of the product,
the social system how people think, feel, and act,
how they behave.
But we also have to pay attention to the economic system
because business is about making money, I'm sorry.
It's an uncomfortable reality
but more money has to come in and goes out.
So there is variance from what could be
in terms of the economic performance
of the system or the process
versus what is.
There may be variance from our principles.
And let's just talk about that for a second.
What does that mean?
Well, if we say for example,
that we believe it is our principle
that decisions should be made at the lowest possible level.
People understand that principle,
they come to believe it, they expect it.
If now decisions are sucked up
and they're not being made at the lowest possible level,
that's a variance from our principle.
If our principle is
that we will involve those who know, those who care,
and those who must act in decision making on our team,
but we don't, we behave differently than that,
we're behaving in a way contrary to our principles,
that erodes the principle, right?
The principle then becomes unimportant,
unbelievable if you will,
so that then becomes a variance to our principles.
There are variances in terms of standards of work
and standards of behavior.
We create standard work and leader standard work
because we believe that's the best possible way
to do something.
So if we're not behaving that way,
if we say that
every day we're gonna be out on the shop floor
talking to people for an hour a day,
half an hour a day, some period of time,
but for three days we don't show up,
that's a variance, right?
Any time we're not behaving according to
standardized definitions of how we ought to behave,
that's a variance.
So those are all forms of variance.
Now I just wanna talk about statistical variation.
And God help you,
I'm not going to give you a course on statistics
and I would not be the right person to do it.
But there's an essential understanding
that even I can convey.
And a simple way to think about it is,
you know, Dr. Deming talked a lot about
standard cause and special causes of variability,
of variation from a mean, right?
And we wanna look at that on a graph.
But an easy way to understand it first is
if you have a gun,
and I know people some people don't like to talk about guns,
but just bear with me for a second
maybe you can think of other example.
But if I have a gun and I vise it down,
I put it in a vise and it's not moving,
you can trust that it's not moving.
And there's a target, a bullseye over there,
and I aim the gun at the target,
and I pull off one shot.
And the shot falls, you know,
six inches to the lower left let's just say,
what do I do?
Do I adjust the gun?
No, pull off another shot.
That shot falls above the bullseye.
Now the bullseye is 200 feet away, okay?
It's not up here, it's 200 feet away.
Each time I pull off the,
even though by the sights I have it aimed right at it,
the bullets don't fall in the same place do they?
Anybody who knows guns knows this is true.
Bullets don't all fall in the same place.
If you pull off 20 shots or 50 shots, what do you see?
You see a pattern, right?
And now somewhere in that pattern there's a middle point.
You can call that middle point the mean.
You know, the mean of where they fell,
the average of where the bullets fell.
But they didn't fall in, they vary.
There's variability in the output of every system.
Every system, a school, a manufacturing plant,
a sales force, software development,
the production the output of that system
is never a straight line.
It's never every bullet falling.
You know, the only way you're gonna get
every bullet to fall into the bullseye,
you better pull the target like right up here,
then they'll all fall.
But that's just bad data, right?
Now if I want,
let's just say that the pattern is like this,
but I want the pattern to be like this.
Maybe I yell at the worker.
Maybe I try to motivate the worker,
the guy pulling the trigger.
Will that help?
No, it won't help.
It won't help.
In fact what he's likely to do is
he then keep adjusting the gun.
You know what's gonna happen?
The pattern's gonna go like this,
variability's gonna increase,
because you're tampering with the system.
You're not improving the system.
The only way you're gonna get the bullets to fall
with less variability,
you're gonna have to redesign the system.
You're gonna have to make
the barrel on the gun longer, okay?
That tends to bring the pattern in.
Now maybe we want a gun with a very short barrel,
okay but then we sacrifice variability, right?
You want less variability,
you add on to the length of the barrel.
Maybe there are other things that you can do.
But just think about the nature of the system,
and the nature of the output of the system,
and the variability that is inherent
in that system and in every system,
and then we think about how to reduce that variability.
Now just look at this graph.
And on this graph we have variation, right?
And here we can see what is variation that is
what Dr. Deming called common cause
and what he calls special cause.
This is how long it takes you to drive to work each morning.
And try it out, you know, you drive to work
and on average let's just say it takes you half an hour
to get from your home to work.
Does it take you exactly a half an hour every day?
Of course not.
It varies, based on what?
Based on the traffic,
based on whether a car broke down
and blocked one of the lanes on the road.
And there are all kinds of things,
based on the weather, right?
Based on, you know, maybe even the the day of the week.
There's always variability in that system like my gun,
like in any system.
Now within the control limits,
you can see what Dr. Deming would call common cause.
Common to the system, normal to that system.
But then, you know, when you see it go up like that,
that's outside the control limits that's special cause.
That's like, there's something really wrong.
Your car broke down or something really happened that where
you know, on average it takes you to half an hour
but it really varies between 20 minutes and 40 minutes
let's just say.
But one day it takes an hour and a half.
Well that's not normal in the system, right?
That's special cause of variation.
Now, why does this matter?
Because you need to be expert
in your process, in your system,
and you need to study that graph.
You need to study what the variability is in your system.
And you need to understand what common cause is, right?
What's normal for that system?
And then you have to ask the question
how do I improve the system, the process,
which may be people or may be machinery, right?
But how do I improve the system to reduce
the common causes of variability?
And when there is special cause,
which is like a defect, right?
Something really went wrong.
What's the cause of that?
And how do I eliminate that?
So study the causes of variation.
And here's a variance analysis worksheet
where you can list the variances that occur.
And with your team, brainstorm.
You know, what is the data telling us?
What are the causes of the variances
or the variation in our system?
And how can we correct for those?
Now that's a very simple explanation
but I want you to think about that,
I want you to start using it as something you can explore,
you know, a lot deeper with
statistical process control and other tools.
But begin to study your data for variability and variation.
